- **🎨 The themes in this project are deliberately *partial* — the base block carries the palette and each theme redefines the handful of values that make it a theme — and nothing was checking the one thing that arrangement depends on**: a theme may override a name, and it must never be the *only* place that name exists. `:root, [data-theme="midnight"]` declares fifty names in the console and the eight themes after it override thirteen each; the public sheet's base declares forty-nine and its eight themes override six (the hue, and nothing else); the hub's declares forty-five and its two light blocks override twenty-two. **Every one of those overrides is a name the base already carries, which is exactly what makes the arrangement safe** — and a single name declared inside a theme block and nowhere else would break that theme's siblings in the silent way this file keeps rediscovering: every other theme drops the declaration and paints nothing, while a sweep for *undeclared* names passes, because the name is declared somewhere. **So the law is one direction — (names a theme declares) ⊆ (names its base declares)** — checked on all three sheets rather than on the console alone. The second half is the hub's own check made general: a sheet's themes must declare *the same* names as each other, so a theme cannot quietly drop an override and render that token in the base's colours. **Which was written first as "at least what the others carry" and could never fail** — an intersection of the theme sets is by definition a subset of every one of them, so the theme that *drops* an override shrinks the intersection and passes it: deleting `--warn` from the forest theme sailed through, the mutation caught it, and the check is equality now. Four mutations were watched to fail — a name declared only inside one theme, in the console and in the public sheet; an override dropped from one theme; and the hub's media-query twin losing a token its chosen-light block has, which `tests/test_hub.py` already pinned for that pair and which is where this law came from. Component-local properties (`.btn { --btn-text: … }`, the landing page's `--lp-*`, the shell's own measurements) and theme-scoped descendant rules (``[data-theme="light"] .aurora``) are deliberately out of scope and named as such in the docstring: they declare next to their own reads, and the undeclared-name sweep is what covers them. 3 new tests (2,548 total, 0 skipped).
- **🧭 `static/dashboard.css` is 1,999 lines that declare three custom properties and read twenty-seven from a file they never mention — which is correct, and which nothing was checking**: the dashboard's layout sheet is loaded *after* `static/meow.css` on every page that has one, and the split is stated in both files' own headers — **the palette is shared, the layout is the page's** — so neither file means anything on its own, and the only thing standing behind each of those twenty-seven names was that nobody had renamed one yet. It is the silence the console's five undefined tokens lived in, one level up: there the sheet was missing its own declarations, and here the missing ones are in a *different file*, which is exactly what a sweep over "the stylesheet" cannot see. **So the law is about the page, and it walks templates rather than files**: every linked sheet, every inline `<style>`, every inline script and every `<script src>`, in the order the browser gets them — and a `var()` with no fallback has to be declared by something that had already arrived when that sheet did, an earlier sheet, an earlier inline block, or a `setProperty()` in a script. Two details keep the check honest rather than merely green: inline JavaScript is read as *setters* and not as text, because a `--name:` inside JS is a string rather than a declaration; and `var(--x, …)` is its own definition, which is how the runtime-set cursor properties satisfy it. **All 28 templates pass, and the interesting half is the four-line assertion that it looked at the right pair** — `/static/meow.css` before `/static/dashboard.css` in the same document, and more than twenty names the dashboard sheet reads and does not declare — so the day somebody inlines that palette, the law says so instead of passing vacuously. Both directions were watched to fail: a read added to `dashboard.css`, and `--border` deleted from `meow.css`, which is the rename in the *other* file that no test could have caught before. The self-contained half of the same law now sweeps two sheets rather than one — `hub/static/hub.css` is generated into pages by `scripts/build_hub.py` and carries its own palette, so it answers to the same sentence (watched to fail the same way). 2 new tests (2,545 total, 0 skipped).
- **🧩 Five custom properties the owner console's stylesheet has never defined — one of them in an SVG `stroke`, where an undefined `var()` means the element is not painted at all — so the Access page's session rows had no background and body-sized metadata, and the Overview's Fleet Reach gauge was an empty ring with "40%" written in the middle of it**: found by reading the *served* pages rather than the source, in the pass that added the Questions page: `--muted`, `--radius-2`, `--surface-2` and `--type-small` are Discord's palette names, carried in with the markup this console replaced, and `--pill-good-text` is a token this sheet never had. **The failure mode is the quiet one, and that is the whole reason nothing in 37 commits since could see it**: `color: var(--muted)` is valid CSS that paints the inherited colour, `font-size: var(--type-small)` is valid CSS that changes nothing, and an undefined name in a `stroke` resolves to `none` — so nothing errored, nothing was logged, and the diff that added each one looked exactly like the diff that works. It was visible only by opening the page: the session list was seven flat rows whose metadata matched its titles in size and brightness, and the second gauge on the Overview drew its track and its percentage with no arc between them. **The fix is the house tokens rather than four new ones**: `--dim` for the two text colours, `--type-meta` for the three type sizes, `--radius-sm` plus the panel-2 glass row the Live page's `.alone__row` already uses, and `--good` for the gauge, which is the colour the Good pill beside it is drawn with — verified in the browser on both pages by reading computed styles before and after (`stroke: none` → `rgb(57, 217, 138)`; `rgba(0, 0, 0, 0)`, `0px`, `16px` → the panel row, `10px`, `13px`). **And it is a law now, because it is the one class of defect no other check can see**: `tests/test_design_laws.py` gains a sixth sentence — *nothing reads a token that does not exist* — as `EveryTokenIsDefined`, which walks every `var()` in the console's sources that carries no fallback and fails on a name the sheet does not declare and `theme.ts` does not `setProperty`. Allowed by definition rather than by exception: the cursor's four are set at runtime *and* always read with a fallback, which is what makes them legitimate. Both mutations were watched to fail — `var(--muted)` put back in one rule, `--pill-good-text` put back in the gauge. 3 new tests (2,543 total, 0 skipped), `tsc --noEmit` clean, bundle re-stamped `c09d09cf81ec`.
- **❓ The questions can now be answered from the website, on the one screen allowed to show who asked — and the console's toast was caught printing Discord's markdown into HTML**: *"the qna cmd isn't there and the site is still fucked look look ui ux … no way to reply to the questions"*, read off a screenshot of the owner console. **The reply path existed and was reachable only from Discord.** `/qna ask` posts the question with nobody's name on it, the owner gets a DM naming the asker with an Answer button, the answer is DMed and never posted — all of that worked, and all of it required an operator to answer a question from a phone card that scrolls away, or to type `/qna reply question:<id>`. The operator was looking at the console when they said there was no way to reply, so the console is where the way now is: a **Questions** page, fourth under *Work*, reading the same table the DM is built from. **The asymmetry is the feature, so the page says it out loud in three places**: the channel sees `anon 4F2A` and the question, the operator sees that *and* `RAJ 1542296506290143349` beside it, and the route is gated by `_require_operator_api` like every other console route — asserted on the *body*, not on the status, because a 403 that still handed back a member's name would pass a status-code test. **A question the channel never saw is marked as such**, with the webhook's own reason (`the webhook did not accept it (HTTPError)`), because "waiting for you" and "the relay refused it" are different findings and only one of them is answered by typing a reply. **And a box that could not be read is never reported as empty** — the reading carries its own `ok`/`because` and the page prints *"the questions could not be read"* above the list, since "nothing is waiting" is the one sentence an operator acts on by doing nothing. **Which the page then said anyway.** Found by reading the built page against its own band: the notice was drawn, and the card underneath it still read **"Nothing is waiting"** with **"0 open · 0 answered"** beside it — the same sentence in a smaller font, printed from a payload whose totals are deliberately zeros that mean *unknown*. The unread branch now replaces the list *and* the shell around it — the heading, the hint, and the counts drawn as the em dash the Access page already uses for a figure nobody took — and `tests/test_console.py` pins it by reading the page's own source, because Python cannot see which branch a `.tsx` takes and the server-side test can only prove the payload said *unknown*, never that the page believed it. **The reading is synchronous on purpose** (`qna.owner_inbox_sync`): a page that could only be drawn while the bot's event loop was running would show nothing during exactly the deploys and restarts an operator opens it to check on. **The delivery is not reimplemented, it is shared.** `cogs.qna.deliver_answer(client, id, answer)` was extracted out of the button's callback, and the route calls it on the bot's own loop: the order inside it — *claim the question, then DM, then put it back if Discord refuses the DM* — is what stops an asker being DMed twice and what stops an answer being lost, so a second copy for the web would have been a second place for that order to be wrong. The console and the Answer button now take one code path, and the sentence that reports the outcome is built once too: `answer_sentence(result)` differs between the surfaces by **two** things and no more — the phrase for where a question goes back to (`/qna inbox` vs *this list*) and whether the asker's name is `**bold**`, the second one added after **the browser showed the first version printing the asterisks**: `✅ Sent to **Meow** as a DM` is the right sentence on a Discord card and four stray characters in an HTML toast where nothing renders markdown. `sent: false` with a sentence is a *working* request (an empty box, a question answered in another tab, a DM Discord refused) and a refusal that changed nothing is reported as one rather than as a failure; only "this process has no bot in it" is a 503, because nothing was attempted. **Two defects came out of building it, both the harness's and both the kind that hide a working feature**: `_PreviewBot.get_user` returned `fetch_user`'s *coroutine* instead of a member — Discord's `get_user` is synchronous — so every reply in a preview failed with `coroutine.send() takes no keyword arguments`, a refusal that looked exactly like Discord's and was entirely the stand-in's; and the seeded questions stored each member's `sub` (`@raj`) as the name where the harness's `label` (`RAJ`) is what the owner's DM renders. **31 new tests** (2,540 total, 0 skipped): the operator's reading (names and counts from the table, unknown-rather-than-empty, a table a restored backup forgot), the shared delivery driven the way the route drives it (two surfaces cannot DM twice), the four route refusals — no bot, no id, no CSRF token, a session scoped to one server — both payload schemas against the server's own answer, and the harness seeding the real table once and being answered through. The two new routes were added to the console's `CONSOLE_ROUTES`/`CONSOLE_WRITE_ROUTES` tuples — the list that exists precisely so a new console route cannot slip past the access tests. `tsc --noEmit` clean, bundle re-stamped `ae0cbec16709`, and the page driven in a browser: three questions read with their names, one answered with the toast reading *"✅ Sent to RAJ as a DM, and anon 0001 is closed. It was not posted anywhere."*, the row leaving the list and the tally moving to *2 open · 1 answered*.
- **🕒 The release notes could be sent *now* or not at all, so a deploy now has a clock: they go out at 03:33 IST, with 04:00 and 04:44 behind them because the free host that keeps this bot alive can be asleep at the first**: *"remove from sending update in meow update channel … send the msg at 3:33 ist if u miss this time then 4 am or 4:44 am ist"*. The notes are the bot talking about itself in a channel it is in, and they fired at whatever hour somebody happened to push — a note at 14:20 is a note in somebody's working day, on every deploy, to every server that ever bound an update channel. There was no request a server could make, either: clearing the binding reads as *not chosen yet* rather than *not wanted*, so `bot_channels.ensure_bound` bound the channel by name again on the next boot, and the home server receives notes through `LEGACY_UPDATE_CHANNEL_ID` even with nothing bound, which is why a checkout running locally can post one into that channel. **So the switch became a switch plus a clock.** `UPDATE_WINDOW_IST` (default `03:33,04:00,04:44`) is the times of day, in IST, a note may go out; `always` is the old behaviour for anyone who wants it; and `SILENT_UPDATES=1` is the other end — not *later* but *never*, which is the only setting that leaves a server's update channel permanently empty. **A held note is owed, not dropped, and that is the property that makes the delay safe.** Nothing is recorded as announced unless Discord accepted the message, so a boot outside the window sends nothing *and* records nothing; the note that goes out at 03:33 is `summarize_changelog(_changelog_since_last(...))`, i.e. everything since that server's last one, so nine deploys in a day are one complete note rather than eight lost ones — a delay is a delay and never a gap. **The clock is `utils/version.py`'s, and the reason it is a fixed +05:30 is the host.** A slim container has no tzdata, so a `zoneinfo` lookup that raises would quietly compute the window in the host's own time — UTC on the deploy, half a day from where it was aimed — and India has had one offset since 1945, so the constant is not an approximation of IST, it is IST. A window stays open for thirty minutes once it arrives, which is what makes a process the monitor woke at 03:34 count as in time, and a window that opens at 23:50 is still open after midnight (the arithmetic is minutes-of-day for exactly that reason). **One rule, two callers.** The boot still calls `_announce_release_notes`, which is now also reached through the client as `bot.announce_release_notes` by `keep_alive.release_window_loop` — the same silence check, the same per-server records, the same legacy channel — because a loop with its own copy of the announcement would be a second way to post a note that the first one cannot see. A retry after a window's failed post waits five minutes rather than thirty seconds, so sixty attempts in one window cannot become the thing the channel remembers about a deploy, and two existing tests now pin the clock wide open: a suite that is about the time of day must not pass or fail depending on what time the suite runs. **22 new tests** (`tests/test_update_window.py`), including the one that reads `render.yaml` to check the window the deploy declares is the window the code runs, and the one that asserts holding a note writes no record. `render.yaml`, `.env.example` and `docs/deployment.md` all carry the pair, with what each end costs.
- **⏳ "Free and online forever" is three things, and only one of them is in this repository — so the one that is not is now written down where it is needed**: *"MAKE IT Free and online forever so even if I leave the project the bot should stay running and not die"*. **Something outside the host has to knock on it.** A free Render instance is stopped after fifteen minutes without an inbound request, and nothing inside the container can prevent that — the timer is on their side, so a self-ping is a bot asking itself to stay awake while the thing measuring it sits somewhere else. The outside knock this deployment already reads is UptimeRobot's free plan (fifty monitors, five-minute intervals, and the same read-only key that turns the dashboard's status panel into history), pointed at `/health`, which is public on purpose and answers counts rather than names for exactly this reason. **The hours are a budget.** 750 instance hours a month against the 730–744 an always-on service spends, so this bot may be the only free service on the account: a second one running the same way suspends both, and the symptom is "the bot died", which is the worst kind of report because it is wrong. The public site and the owner console are on Cloudflare Pages, which has no such budget. **And the disk is temporary, which is survivable rather than fatal**: Render wipes the filesystem on every deploy, and the database on it is restored from Supabase at boot, snapshotted hourly, and — the part that matters for a host that sleeps — flushed to Supabase on `SIGTERM`, the signal Render sends *before* it stops an idle instance, so a nap is a save rather than a loss. The shrunken-database guard is what stops a wiped local file from being uploaded over the good copy after a bad boot. All three are now `docs/deployment.md`'s *Free, and online without you*, with the parts this does **not** cover said out loud (a hard kill with no warning, and a deployment where the Supabase pair was never set). The README's troubleshooting row for "free host sleeps/stops" now names the monitor instead of pointing at a document that never mentioned one.
- **🖱️ The Cursor panel never opened on the owner site — the rail was told to clip, the panel was told to open *outside* the rail, and both instructions landed on the same element**: *"The Fix aren't there the cursor panel is fucked in owner site"*. `.rail` clips (`overflow: hidden`, added so its seven nav groups and pinned footer could not paint over the page beside them), while `.customizer-popover` was `position: absolute; left: calc(100% + 12px)` — deliberately *outside* the rail, which is the one place a clipping ancestor does not allow. Clicking **Cursor** drew nothing at all, so the control read as broken rather than as a gesture with a hidden result. **The panel now leaves the clipping context instead of arguing with it**: it renders through a React portal on `document.body` with `position: fixed`, placed from the button's own `getBoundingClientRect()` — measured rather than hardcoded, which also covers the case the old `@media (max-width: 860px)` rule existed for, because on a narrow window there is no room beside the rail and it opens above the button instead — and re-placed on resize and scroll so it cannot be left behind. Console rebuilt, `tsc --noEmit` clean, source stamp `cd3108015e0b`.
- **❓ `/qna` — an anonymous question box, where the promise is one sentence and the code is the sentence**: *"Build the /qna feature: an anonymous question relay driven by an environment-variable webhook, a way for you to reply that DMs the asker, and the asker's name visible only to you so the anonymity promise stays true."* A member runs `/qna ask`, types their question into a form, and the question appears in the server's channel posted by a **webhook** (`QNA_WEBHOOK_URL`) whose name is *Anonymous* — not a disguised member, an absent one, with `allowed_mentions` empty so a question cannot ping `@everyone`. The owner gets a **DM about the same question that does name the asker**, with an Answer button on it; the answer is **DMed to the asker** and never posted, because an answer in the channel would tell everybody that somebody asked, which is the one fact the asker was promised away. **The asymmetry is enforced, not described.** The public body is built by `utils/qna.relay_payload` from a closed field list — the question's id, its text, the time — and the test builds a payload from a row whose asker is unmistakable and fails if the name or the id appears anywhere in what the webhook is handed; the name is rendered in exactly two places, the owner's DM and `/qna inbox`, and both refuse anyone who is not `OWNER_USER_ID` (checked in the callback, because Discord has no permission bit that means "this one account"). The public handle is **derived** — `anon 4F2A` is the question's id in hex — so there is no second identifier to correlate and nothing that can collide. **Three failure modes are answered rather than assumed.** The question is written down *before* the relay is attempted, so a webhook that refuses the post leaves a row saying so instead of losing it, and the asker is told the box refused it; the answer is **claimed before the DM is sent** (one conditional `UPDATE`, so two clicks on an old card cannot DM somebody twice) and **put back in the inbox** if the DM itself fails, because an answer that never arrived has not been given; and an unset `QNA_WEBHOOK_URL` makes `/qna ask` say which setting is missing rather than doing nothing, which is the state that reads as a broken bot. **Two Discord mechanics decide the shapes here, both read out of the installed library rather than guessed at**: a modal's submit is dispatched *only* to a modal the client already knows about, and `send_modal` registers it by `custom_id` — so the answer form cannot use a fixed `custom_id` (two forms open at once would overwrite each other and one would answer the wrong question) and the question id rides in that field instead; and a button on an old message is dispatched by `custom_id` once the process that sent it is gone, so the Answer button carries **no state at all** and the card's *message id* is what finds the question — one view registered at boot answers every card ever sent, however old. The DM form is registered for dispatch by the modal itself, which is why this feature adds no `on_interaction` listener and no per-question view. **43 new tests** (`tests/test_qna.py`, 2471 total, 0 skipped), the registry now reads **15 systems and 96 commands** (`docs/discord-directory.md` follows it, as that file's own guard requires), and `PRIVACY.md` §1 plus `docs/legal/data-requests.md` name the row — the question, the answer, and who asked, kept until deleted, rendered only to the owner.
- **🖼️ The operator console's own logo was a broken image on five of its nine themes — and the reason it could happen is that a component was deciding for itself which files exist**: *"check the site i think its fucked all 5 site the logo aren't loading"*. `Shell.tsx` drew the brand mark as `/static/logo-<theme>.svg`, which is a *promise* that somebody drew that file; the theme vocabulary grew from four to nine in `588d41f` and only four marks were ever made, so **Aurora Borealis, Cosmic Nebula, Cyber Plasma, Sunset Flare and Synthwave** each asked for a file that does not exist and were handed the browser's broken-image glyph, next to the bot's own name, in the rail. **There is nothing wrong with the missing marks** — four themes having their own logo and five using the default is a perfectly good design. What was wrong was the console assuming, theme by theme, that a file it never checked was there. The path now comes from `logoFor(theme)` in the module that owns the theme vocabulary, so **one door** decides, and the rule is *checked rather than trusted*: `tests/test_console_brand.py` walks `THEMES` against `static/` **in both directions** and fails on a theme that has a mark but is not declared, or is declared without a file — proven by temporarily declaring `aurora` and watching the guard name it as the offender. Two halves came with it. The `<img>` gained a guarded `onError` fallback, because the *static* owner build is a second host that could have failed to copy a mark and a missing file should degrade to the real logo rather than to a glyph. And `scripts/build_owner.py` now **discovers** `logo-*.svg` instead of listing four by hand — a listed file is a promise somebody has to remember, and that is the exact shape of this bug. The outbound law that forbids a component building an image URL by hand was widened to admit `logoFor()` *and* strengthened: it is the second door into an `src`, so it is asserted the way `iconSrc` is — one interpolation, and it is the theme id. `tsc --noEmit` clean, bundle re-stamped `05d318ba0320`. 2428 tests + 1312 subtests pass, 7 new.
- **📻 The radio was six stations, so the fallback was a formality — it is now 122 across 54 genres and ten languages, reachable by a search rather than a menu, and every one of them was fetched before it was written down**: *"in 2nd one upgrade it add more things more features more station and many more like more types instrumental music type language more etc…"*. Radio is the answer to *"the platforms will not serve this host"*, and a six-item answer is not an answer: somebody who wanted Turkish pop, baroque, chiptune or **a language they actually speak** was still told no. **The picker had to stop being a picker first.** Discord caps a command's fixed choices at 25 and renders them in declaration order, so a menu is a catalogue nobody can find *Turkish* in — which is why the station option is now an **autocomplete over the words people have** (name, genre, language, mood), with `/music stations` as the browse command for a reader who does not know a word to type. Moods are searched in the words people *use*: nobody searches for `focus`, they search for *study*, so a deliberately small synonym table maps one to the other and is discounted a rank, so it can never displace a literal match. **Two rules govern what may be added, both from failures this project has already had.** Every URL was **fetched before it was written down** — 122 live out of roughly 200 candidates, with a 404, a DNS failure and an HTML error page all rejected — and every station is named **what the stream calls itself**, not what a directory called it: the pass that built this found the entry filed under *baroque* was a Bee Gees station and the one under *classical* was Qur'an recitation, both fine stations and neither what the label promised. **And the weekly self-check could not see 91 of the 122.** `utils/media_health` decides whether a literal is media partly by its **extension**, and a radio stream frequently has none — `https://stream.laut.fm/lofi` is a host and a word — so the module written to stop dead links rotting silently would have declined to check most of the new catalogue, which is its own bug one level down. The fix is a **declared** `STREAM_HOSTS` list (ports included, because they are part of the comparison), which leaves the scanner's contract untouched and puts every station into the probe by *having been written down*. **`/music radio` with no argument now replays what the server chose last** — a new `music_radio` row, written best-effort because remembering is not worth a failure — and the first-ever invocation picks one and *says that it picked* instead of pretending the choice was the member's. **Separately, the failure that named the wrong cause is fixed.** A refusal was being raised over requests that had *no recording to play*: *"YouTube is refusing this host's address right now"* about a search SoundCloud answered with four edits and Audius with three remixes — a true sentence pointing at the wrong problem, since nothing was wrong with this host's reach. The message now names both facts, and adds the second clause only when it is true of **every** provider asked (*"and the other sources have nothing but edits, covers or remixes of it"* / *"…do not have it"*), because a claim this code cannot support is worse than a shorter sentence. Anything that fails as `blocked` also now names the door that still opens, since the radio touches no provider at all. 2422 tests + 1303 subtests pass, 36 new in `tests/test_radio.py`.
- **🎧 The bot says *brb* when music takes it out of somebody's room — and the owner console's rail can no longer draw itself on top of the page**: *"fix the ui ux of the msg and other things and send a msg like brb when person plays music in vc b"*. **The message is about a silence, not a missing feature.** The two voice features share one connection because one Discord client sits in one voice channel — so when somebody in *another* room presses play, music moves the bot out of the room it was sitting in, and the person left behind watches it leave and is told nothing. Unsaid, that reads as *"it left because of me"* when the truth is a two-room errand it was asked to run. So it says so, in its own voice: *brb — somebody asked for music in another room, so I'm on speaker duty*, with a footer saying when it is back and who can turn the behaviour off. **Three deliberate refusals in one small card.** Nothing is sent when the music started in the room the bot was *already in* — that person is now less alone than they were, and a goodbye for a song playing in their own channel is noise, so the condition is the channel *changing* rather than music starting at all. Nothing is sent to a server that chose silent arrivals, because it asked for silence and the greeting's own setting is the place that decision already lives. And it names **no command**, held to the same rule the greeting's footer is: it lands in front of whoever was alone, and the switch is a Manage Server command Discord hides from their picker entirely, so every variant and the footer are now asserted to contain no slash token. **The console bug was a column that could not hold itself.** `.rail` is a fixed `100vh` box with no clipping, and its content is taller than that on a laptop — seven nav groups plus a footer pinned to the bottom — so the footer *escaped the box* and painted over the grid's other column, which is exactly how the appearance swatches and the Cursor button ended up sitting on top of the Overview's own action buttons. The fix is clipping rather than a taller box, since the rail has to fit the viewport: the column no longer lets anything out, the brand and the footer are fixed-size, and the page list — the one flexible child — scrolls inside it instead of pushing the footer out. Console rebuilt, `tsc --noEmit` clean, source stamp `3a10af3a86f4`. 2386 tests + 930 subtests pass, 4 new.
- **🚪 The card asked *which of these did you mean* and then failed to play the one that was picked, because the menu came from a **search** — and a search is the half of the exchange a hostile provider still answers**: *"Why its showing option when I gave him the link and it takes soo long after selcting and error"*, over a Spotify link that resolved, a shortlist of YouTube uploads, and a `DownloadError` after choosing one. **The bot had already said the answer out loud** — *YouTube is refusing this host's address right now* — and then offered a YouTube-only menu, which is the same fact ignored twice in a row. The reason the two are compatible is that they are two different requests: a flat search is cheap and unauthenticated and keeps working, while only the **stream** extraction is refused. So a provider can answer a search and have nothing it can hand over, and the chain returned its shortlist the moment it saw candidates — it never checked that the provider could play any of them. **Now the menu is only offered once its provider has proved it can hand over its own top choice.** That is not extra work: the alternatives carry no stream URL, so choosing one cost an extraction *anyway*, after the member had waited and after the card had promised — the same extraction, moved earlier, where it discovers a refusal while there is still a provider left to try and leaves the likeliest choice already resolved and fresh by the time it is picked. A provider already inside its refusal cooldown is skipped outright rather than probed, since that answer was established minutes ago and re-establishing it costs seconds on every song. **Building this surfaced a worse one behind it.** SoundCloud's answer to a mainstream single was a **30-second preview** of the real upload plus four edits — a *Slowed*, a *Piano & String Version* remix, an *Acoustic Version* and a *Mashup* — and after the unusable preview was excluded, three of those wrong recordings were offered as a choice. A set made **entirely** of other recordings is not a shortlist to pick from; it is a provider with nothing, and the next one is asked. So with YouTube refused, the member now gets one sentence in about **4.6 seconds** — naming the platform that is refusing the host, which is the fact they can act on — instead of a four-item menu of remixes that ends in an error. Worth writing down plainly, because it is the question the report really asks: **a Spotify address cannot play anything by itself.** Spotify hands over metadata and no audio, so a link there is a metadata fetch followed by a *search*, which is why a link can ask at all. 2382 tests + 930 subtests pass, 4 new.
- **🌐 A refused address now has a third answer that needs no account at all — an outbound proxy, applied to **both halves** of the request, because a provider signs its stream URL for whoever asked for it**: *"Add an outbound proxy setting so yt-dlp can reach YouTube from this datacenter host without cookies"*, following the pass that measured the refusal itself. Cookies need a throwaway login and a PO token needs a token provider; a proxy asks nothing of an account, which is what makes it the answer for an operator who has neither. **The half-fix is the whole problem.** A ``googlevideo`` URL is signed for the address that asked for it, so a proxy that runs the extraction and not the fetch hands back a URL *this host cannot open* — the stream resolves, the card names the track, and then nothing is heard, which reads as a provider being unreliable rather than as a setting applied to one half of a pair. So the same setting is handed to ffmpeg as ``-http_proxy`` on the input side, where an input option belongs, and the pairing is asserted rather than assumed. **It is deliberately not applied to everything.** Radio and the credential-free catalogue are the two sources that do not refuse this host, and radio is the fallback that exists to keep playing when the extractor cannot — so it is the one thing that must not acquire a new way to fail. The rule is derived from the provider that actually served the stream instead of being listed, which also keeps ``-http_proxy`` off inputs that are not HTTP at all, where an unknown protocol option is an error in its own right. **A SOCKS URL is declined with its reason rather than half-honoured**, and that is a measured decision: ffmpeg has no SOCKS support in any version, so a SOCKS proxy would carry the extraction and leave the fetch behind — precisely the failure above, arriving from a setting that looked configured. The reason is printed once per process and carried on the operators' sources reading next to a boolean; the URL itself never is, because it holds a credential, so it is a declared secret and the module gained a redaction helper for anything it prints. **The law guarding this surface caught the new work, and then caught something older**: a setting read through a constant is invisible to the config audit's search, and ``YTDL_SOURCE_ORDER`` had been readable, undeclared **and** unfindable all at once — an operator reading the report was never told that setting existed. Both names are now spelled out where they are read. **Proven with a real proxy rather than a mocked one**: ffmpeg fetched through a local one from an origin address that is not reachable at all, exited 0, and the proxy logged the absolute-form request line, which is the evidence that it was used *as* a proxy. 2378 tests + 930 subtests pass, 13 new.
- **🔊 The echo had a number, and the number was the channel's own bitrate: every song that was not already Opus was being decoded and re-encoded down to **64 kbps** on any server without a boost, discarding more than half of a recording that had arrived intact — measured at **8.9 dB** of fidelity thrown away, and now recovered**: *"the quality is like echo and shitty ass — find a method that provides highest quality and best music and no error"*, over a now-playing card reading `ffmpeg re-encode [Normal (studio clean)]`. Three separate things were wrong on the one path this deployment actually plays through, and the first of them is a one-line condition that had never been exercised. **The pass-through path was unreachable.** It is the whole high-quality route — the provider's own Opus packets handed on untouched, no encoder in the loop at all — and it required the source to be *no louder than the channel*. A channel's bitrate is where Discord's own client should encode and it is **64 kbps on an ordinary server**, while every audio-only Opus song is ~130 kbps, so the test failed for every song and re-encoded all of them down to 64. Sending the provider's own packets costs nothing and cannot sound worse than re-encoding them, so the clause is gone. **The encoder was tuned for a phone call, because the library it comes from is a voice library.** `discord.py` writes `-fec true -packet_loss 15` into every `FFmpegOpusAudio` command line and never sets `-application`, and the in-band redundancy it is asking for is carried by libopus's **speech** path. Measured against a musical signal at 96 kbps: `-application audio` **−43.83 dB**, adding discord.py's FEC **−39.53 dB**, and `-application voip` **−36.97 dB** — about **7 dB** of avoidable loss, of which turning FEC off removed the whole 4.3 dB share even with `-packet_loss 15` still present. `utils/player.py` now appends a `MUSIC_ENCODER` (`-application audio -frame_duration 20 -vbr on -compression_level 10 -fec false -packet_loss 0`) *after* discord.py's flags, where ffmpeg's last-occurrence-wins rule makes them the ones that take effect. **And the encoder was told to aim at the channel rather than at the source.** `encode_bitrate()` now takes the source's own rate as the target, never under what the channel asks for, never past 256 kbps — a 160 kbps recording is no longer halved before libopus has done anything else, because encoding above the source cannot restore what is gone and encoding below it throws away what is not. End to end on the exact command line, against a 160 kbps source on a 64 kbps channel: the old path error was **−42.95 dB**, the new one **−51.83 dB**. **Two further defects came out of the same reading.** A second `-af` flag does not chain with the first, it *replaces* it — so a filter chosen with `/music filter` vanished in silence whenever the volume was not at 100%, and the filtergraph is now built as one comma-joined chain; and the engine line on the now-playing card names the bitrate it settled on (`pass-through (131 kbps, untouched)` / `ffmpeg encode [Normal (studio clean)] 160 kbps`), because "why does this sound bad" should be answerable from Discord and not from a container log. **Separately, a right-sounding song that was the wrong recording:** with YouTube refusing this host the second provider becomes the first one asked, and SoundCloud's answer for the studio single was *`we can't be friends (wait for your love) [Acoustic Version]`* — so an **arrangement** now counts as a different recording in the scorer's disqualifying markers, alongside `cover` and `remix`, word-bounded and skipped when the query asks for that flavour, so *"Ariana Grande acoustic"* still finds one. Worth being plain about what this does and does not buy: only YouTube serves Opus, so the pass-through half helps exactly when YouTube can be reached, and on a host YouTube refuses the whole gain is the second and third fixes — not discarding a source's bitrate, and not padding it with speech-path redundancy.
- **📣 The events that cannot be posted to a channel now arrive anyway — a server added, a join wave, and a flood against the site each go out over a webhook that does not need the gateway, silently, and the fields that leave are the ones the policy publishes**: *"connect web to <webhook> so when someone comes or add bot it sends a silent msg in that channel so we are ready for anytype of ddos or anything else. Basic Details like OS Ip Etc… everything should be under tos and privacy policy and legal docs in legal words"*. The three events worth waking somebody for are exactly the three a channel inside a server **cannot** receive: a server that has *just* added the bot is in none of that server's channels, a down gateway is the reason the web process is answering at all, and a request flood arrives at the web surface, which has no gateway to begin with. So there is a second channel — `ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL` — and it is deliberately the plainest transport here: one HTTP POST, on a daemon thread, from a module with no dependency but `urllib`. **Four properties, each because the alternative is worse.** It is **silent** (Discord's suppress-notifications flag), so the channel can be watched without paging anybody who is asleep. It is **bounded** — one message per kind per 30 seconds, 120 per process — because a flood is precisely where an unbounded notifier is worst: the thing watching the flood must not be the amplifier. It **never raises and never blocks**, so a slow receiver cannot hold a page open. And it is **off until configured and says so**: an unset URL is a supported state, and the Status page reports it as a *missing setting* rather than as a quiet success, which is the version of a disabled feature that costs somebody a night. **The disclosure is the feature's specification, not a paragraph beside it.** What travels is a closed list — the event kind; the requesting address and the client's own user-agent string, which is what names an operating system; the path and the coarse country; the counts that make an event legible; and a server's name and id — published in **`PRIVACY.md` §5** and **`TERMS.md` §12** in the documents' own register (lawful basis: legitimate interest in the integrity and availability of the service; limited to what identifying and repelling the request requires; not profiled, not sold, not shared for advertising, not used for training), and **asserted as an exact key set by `tests/test_alert_webhook.py`** so a new field is a failing test rather than a silent policy change. The join alert names the inviter and **how old that account is** — a hundred invites from accounts minutes old is what a raid looks like, and it is the only signal that separates an invitation from one. Proven live against the operator's own channel before it was committed to.
- **🚪 Music no longer dies when a provider refuses the host — because the search now has a third door that asks for no credential at all, and the card that used to say *"a provider refused"* now names the platform that did**: *"Most Important Still not working to a perma fix"*, over a `/play` of a Spotify link ending in *"Music: the provider refused this host"*. Two providers is a choice between two doors that are both shut on a datacenter address — YouTube answers one with a bot check, SoundCloud with a 403 — so an operator who has configured neither cookies nor a PO token had nowhere left to go, and the previous attempt (a client ladder) could only help a host YouTube still tolerates. **The answer is a provider that never needed tolerating**: [Audius](https://audius.co)'s open API hands over a **direct audio URL** for a catalogue track and asks for no account, no cookie and no player client — no bot check to pass, no signature to decipher — so a refused host now plays the song instead of explaining itself, verified end to end with YouTube *and* SoundCloud forced to refuse. It is third rather than first because its catalogue is narrower for a mainstream request, and being third means it is reached exactly when the other two fail. **The diagnosis was wrong before the fix**, and separately from it: the chain raised *"the last failure"*, while `source_order` deliberately moves a refusing provider to the back — so a SoundCloud refusal was reported as YouTube refusing a platform that had barely been asked, and *that false sentence is what both the member's card and the operator's alert were built from*. A refusal now outranks a provider that simply found nothing, and it is raised **named**. **The client ladder had gone stale in silence**: of the thirteen clients yt-dlp 2026.08 knows, its second rung did not exist — `tv_embedded` had been removed upstream, so the rung had become a copy of the first, spending a full extraction on every already-failing search to learn what the first attempt had just said, and nothing anywhere reported it. The ladder is now filtered against the extractor's own client table, so the next rename costs nothing instead of costing speed on exactly the hosts the ladder exists for. `/music doctor` and the extractor panel gained **the doors that are left and the order they will be knocked on** — the reading that stops "the provider refused this host" from being the whole story — and the operator's remedy now says which credential is short and which platform needs it.
- **🫂 The card that arrives uninvited stopped naming a command its reader cannot run, and the Overview stopped being four numbers and a great deal of space**: *"why u showing cmd to use in multiple cmd to normal user? they can't even use"* and *"Add More Features Its Kinda Empty"*, over the companion's own greeting and the dashboard's Overview. **The card was the real defect**: `/company` is declared with the **Manage Server** audience, so Discord hides it from the picker of nearly everybody the card lands in front of — a footer carrying `/company mode:Off` was therefore a card that read as broken to the only person it addressed. It now says *who can act* and names no syntax at all, and the rule is asserted as a rule rather than a phrasing: **no slash token, no option name, anywhere in the card**. The copy warmed with it, in the bot's own voice — *"sat down next to you. Take your time, I'm not going anywhere"*, and the stream field stopped explaining `self_stream` and the missing Go Live API *to the person who was just noticed*, which was true and useless in equal measure. **The Overview answered four questions about the deployment and none about the server**, which is why it read as empty; it now carries **who is in a room and who is in one on their own** (read off the gateway, reporting what it could read rather than painting a zero over a cold process), **what the player is doing** with the provider order behind it, and the four destinations people actually come here for. The Server page gained **what the bot's own role lets it do here** — read from `guild.me`, costing no request — because the failure that panel exists to pre-empt is a button that reports success and changes nothing: the bot was invited without *Manage Channels*, or its role sits below the one it is changing.
- **🎧 A song that the provider refuses is now played from somewhere else instead of dying — and the card that used to say *"that stream was cut off"* over a provider that never handed over a stream now says what actually happened**: *"i want smt that doesn't die"*, over a screenshot of the exact failure: a track found, a disambiguation menu answered, and then `DownloadError` — *"the provider could not be read"* — with the queue moving on. **The cause is a network, not a bug.** yt-dlp negotiates a stream through one of several *player clients*, and a datacenter IP is refused by the default one: the same commit that plays every song on a laptop resolves nothing on the host, which is why this survived every test and every local run. So the extractor now climbs a **ladder of client sets** — yt-dlp's own choice first (the only rung that negotiates audio-only Opus), then the clients that keep answering when the default is refused — and it keeps climbing until one returns *one URL a player can open*, which is the distinction that had been missing: a rung can answer with 47 formats and still have nothing playable, because the selected format is a video/audio pair that would need muxing. The rung that worked is remembered for the process, so a host that has settled does not re-learn it per song; a *network* failure stops the climb immediately, because four more extractions cannot give a host a route it does not have; and an operator's `YTDL_PO_TOKEN` is the first rung rather than the only one, which it used to be — a token the provider had stopped honouring disabled every fallback, so the one deployment configured to cope was the one that died first. **And before a track is given up on, the same song is looked for somewhere else.** SoundCloud is the second provider because this resolver already understands it end to end, and because it is markedly harder to refuse a datacenter IP on; the recording is identified by its **own known length** rather than by another search's confidence margin, so a four-way tie between same-named uploads is an answer instead of silence. The card *says* it substituted, because an unexplained swap is the wrong-song bug wearing a different hat, and a rescue that cannot itself be streamed is no rescue — so it is not offered. **Four defects came out with it.** The fault card had one headline for every kind of failure and printed *"That stream was cut off"* over a `DownloadError` — nothing had been cut off, and the sentence sent whoever read it to look at the wrong thing, so each kind now has its own headline, colour and advice, and the substitute is drawn as information rather than as a warning; SoundCloud identity was the **numeric API id**, which as a path resolves to a *user*, so `soundcloud.com/1856469195` 404s (the rescue reached that on its first real run) and now the page address is the identity; `watch_url` rebuilt the same broken URL from that id; and the weekly media check gained a **third bucket** for things that are neither dead nor unchecked — an extractor answering *through a fallback* is working today and one provider change from working not at all, and filing it as dead would send somebody to fix a link that resolves. `Probe` reports which client answered and whether it had to climb, `/music doctor` prints it, the console shows `climbing` as its own status, and the weekly pass reads it — but stays silent about it, because a card every week about something that works is how a channel gets muted. 2320 tests pass, 20 new.
- **🛡️ A server's staff can moderate from the website and the operator can act on a server as a whole, and both surfaces are built on one rulebook rather than two — because the alternative was Discord's own audit log crediting the bot for what a person asked for**: *"add options on dashboard — 1. manage members like kicking, ban, remove etc… 2. manage whole server like locking down and etc… and for owner dashboard, manage server — can kick myself, or ban that server, and other things"*. **Two new dashboard pages, from the nav down.** `/dashboard/members` is a roster — searchable, with the ban list beside it, because the member you want to unban is by definition not in the roster — and one action form: warn, time out, remove a timeout, kick, ban, unban, clear warnings, each with a reason that reaches the member and is the record. `/dashboard/server` is the whole server at once: a **lockdown** that denies Send Messages, thread creation and reactions to `@everyone` in every channel the bot can manage, a **lift** that puts back *exactly* what each channel had before, and **slowmode** applied to every channel with Discord's own ceiling clamped rather than refused. **The rulebook is one module, not two copies of it.** `utils/moderation_actions.py` is the guard `/kick` has always used — you may not act on an administrator, the owner, or somebody at your own level, and an actor whose own member could not be read is *refused* rather than waved through — and the cog is now a thin caller of it, so a kick from a phone and a kick from a webpage take the same code path to the same audit row. `utils/server_control.py` holds the plan/apply split for the bulk changes: the plan is pure and needs nothing but the guild object (which is why the page can still say what a lockdown *would* do when the ledger could not be read), and the apply writes the ledger **before** it touches a channel, because a bulk permission change that failed to record itself would have no undo. **The website's record exists because Discord's does not name the person.** A web action credits the *bot* in Discord's audit log — the bot is what performed it — so `web_actions` is the only place the signed-in administrator's identity is attached to what happened, and the page prints the last twelve. **The console gained the four things only the operator can do**: republish a server's command list, leave it, leave *and block* it, and take it off the blocklist — with the blocklist enforced on the way in rather than only on the way out, because a list that a re-invite can walk around is a note to self rather than a rule. **Three defects came out of building it, and all three were found by the suite rather than by looking.** The panels vanished their own controls when a read failed, so the ids the page's script reaches for did not exist — the copy is now *inert and says why*, controls present, disabled, with the reason in the field beside them; **the dashboard's rail became nine pages and the design law that said "seven" was a cap rather than a rule**, so it is chunked into groups the way the operator's rail already was, with the same arithmetic asserted (no group over five, five groups at most, every page placed in the order it was declared); and the preview harness had been rendering an empty `<nav>` on this page since the rail was split, because it never passed the page list, so the one tool that exists to look at the rail was the one place it could not be seen. **Considered and not done:** a DJ-style permission model for who may moderate from the website. Today it is the session's own principal (the person must actually hold the Discord permissions for what they ask), which is the same rule the slash commands apply and needs no new settings table — a "moderators may use the web panel" toggle would be a second way to express one permission.
- **🎵 Music is back, and it is a rebuild rather than a restoration — because the archived cog's three complaints were three *mechanisms*, not three bugs: it searched again for a song it had already found, it mistook an aborting stream for a finished one, and it never asked whether the host could play at all**: *"We will start the work on music bot again — create a plan how to do that so it will work perfectly."* **"It plays a different song"** was identity: the archived cog kept the search *query* on each track and, when a stream URL expired, ran that query again — and a search is not a lookup, so the refresh could return a different upload than the card the member was reading, mid-song, where nobody could see it happen. Every track now carries a **stable identity** (`provider` + that provider's own id, `utils/media_source.py`), re-resolution asks for *that recording*, and the search happens exactly once, at request time. **"The music is short"** was the same class of bug one layer down: a stream that dies reaches `discord.py` as a finished track, so the queue used to move on in silence — and the archived `FFMPEG_BEFORE_OPTIONS` (`-err_detect ignore_err`) is precisely what hid it. The player now builds **one** ffmpeg command line in one place (no such flag), counts through a **generation counter** so a stale `after()` callback cannot advance a queue that has already moved, and compares how much actually played against what the provider declared — a track that ends at 30% of a declared four minutes is reported as **cut off**, with the engine's own last words, to the member *and* to the operator, because "the song was really short" and "this host was cut off" are different facts and only one of them is somebody's playlist. **"Music is broken here"** is `utils/voice_caps.py`, which probes **before** anything joins a channel: an ffmpeg binary (system, then `imageio-ffmpeg`'s static build), an Opus library *if there is one*, and `davey` — which this `discord.py` refuses to open a voice connection without, so a host missing it looked exactly like a server where nobody had asked for a song. Every missing reason is printed separately, because each one is a different fix, and the boot line, `/music doctor`, `/api/voice-debug` and the owner console's new **Music panel** all read that same probe. **The command surface is the other half of the plan, and it is arithmetic**: Discord allows **100 top-level chat-input commands per scope** and this bot publishes 88 roots, so the archived cog's twelve roots could not come back as they were. `/play` keeps its own slot — the one name people type from memory — and the other sixteen live under **one** `/music` group, which is why the registry now reads **14 systems, 95 commands**. **What is deliberately not there is as considered as what is**: `/music radio` streams an HTTP station directly and never touches the extractor, so it keeps working on a host the platforms have blocked (the one failure that cannot be designed away, and the reason `YTDL_COOKIES_B64` / `YTDL_PO_TOKEN` exist as documented answers rather than an escape hatch); `/music 247` remembers its seat in the `music_247` table and reclaims it on boot; the queue lives in the process and a restart empties it; nothing is recorded and nothing is stored per member. **And the one thing that could rot silently forever got a probe**: `yt-dlp` stays installed, importable and reporting a version while answering every search with *"Sign in to confirm you're not a bot"*, and every link in the tree can be healthy on that day — so `utils/media_health.py` grew a **third kind of target** that asks the extractor for a known recording weekly (`media_source.self_check`, Blender's own upload, `YTDL_PROBE_TARGET` to override), and reads the failure for what it is: a provider refusing this host is a **finding**, a host with no route out is **`unchecked`** at the same rule as any other "could not ask", and the version travels inside the reason because *one release behind* is the common case and invisible without it. `media_health.extractor_state()` is the cheap read of that verdict for the console and `/health` (no network call, and an honest *not asked since this process started*), while `/music doctor` and `/api/voice-debug` ask **now** — a version string is not evidence. Music also reaches every web surface: the public `templates/music.html` (command list read out of the registry, so the page cannot advertise a command the bot does not publish), the hub's registry-driven catalogue, and the console panel that draws capability, player and extractor in one card. **2,245 tests pass, 0 skipped** — the two long-standing skips are gone, because the music suites came back rewritten against the new modules (`tests/test_music.py`, `tests/test_music_matching.py`, 8 new cases in `tests/test_media_health.py` and a new `tests/test_voice_caps.py`); `tsc` clean, the console bundle rebuilt and re-stamped, and all three static builds pass. Also in this pass: `.dockerignore` no longer hides `assets/` (the fonts `utils/card.py` draws the now-playing card with — an image missing them renders in Pillow's bitmap font and says nothing), and the Dockerfile installs `ffmpeg` + `libopus0` as an *upgrade* rather than a precondition, since the native runtime can already decode and encode.
- **🗜️ Three complaints from one afternoon of using the console were one broken switch, one reading nobody took, and one command hidden by a rule the card did not mention**: *"company cmd isn't there", a red toast reading *"the server answered something this build does not understand (`guild_id` should be string, is number)"*, and *"there are ppl online but not showing on dashboard"*. **The toast was the switch and the switch was the toast**: `/api/toggle` and `/api/toggle-all` answered with `guild_id` as a JSON *number* while the console's schema — the boundary every payload crosses before a page sees it — says string. A snowflake that crosses as a number comes back as a different number, so the type is a law, not a style choice; and because the check throws *before* the page can use the answer, the failure was not cosmetic: the click landed in the caller's `catch`, the knob sprang back, and the follow-up that reads whether Discord was actually told to republish never ran — which is how a module could be on in the database, on in this console, and absent from Discord's picker with nothing on screen to say which. Both routes answer text now, the response carries **`republish`** (whether the bot was up to hear the request at all), and a switch that could not queue one says *saved, but the command list was not republished — try again in a moment* instead of a green toast about a command nobody can type yet. **"People online but not showing" was the one thing the Live page promised never to do**: `_live_presence` counted `Member.status`, on the theory that the attribute is absent without the Presence intent. It is not — discord.py always has it, and Discord answers **offline for every member** — so the card printed *1 online, 35 offline* for a server full of people, the one online being the bot, whose presence its own process sets. Five figures, none of them a reading. The guard is now the **intent** (`presences` *and* `members`, since presences only arrive for the members a client tracks), `read.presence` goes false, and the page draws the dash and the switch that turns it on. And the intent is now askable: `ENABLE_PRESENCE_INTENT` goes through the same portal check every other privileged intent does (`portal_intents` reads Discord's own application flags, `drop_ungranted_intents` drops what the portal has not granted — asking for one it has not makes Discord refuse the *whole* connection), declared in `config_audit` and `.env.example`, off by default, with the Live page naming it in the gap notice. **"/company isn't there" was the honest half of a card that overpromised**: the greeting's footer told whoever was alone to run `/company mode:Off`, but `/company` is declared with the Manage Server audience — so Discord *hides it from the picker* of a member without that permission, and the card was pointing at a command its reader could not see. The footer says *an administrator can stop this* now, the console's publish line says out loud that Discord's own client caches the command list, and a module switch that adds commands reports whether the republish was heard. **2,158 tests pass, 2 skipped** — the new ones are the kind that fail on the mutation: `TheConsoleSchemaIsWhatTheServerAnswers` reads each schema out of `payload.ts` and compares it field by field with what the server actually answers (watched to fail by putting the raw int back), `_live_presence` is asserted absent with the intent off, present with both intents on, and absent with presence-but-no-members, and the footer is pinned to the audience it is addressed to.
- **🤖 The bot's own moderation decisions had no record and no appeal — and while checking the tree against the seven reference documents, the smallest thing it found was 10px type**: a pass over *"those stuff … according to its job"*, where "those" is the Frontend/UIUX, Backend/API, Security, Accessibility, Privacy, Legal and Bot-Platform references. **The real find was the bot's automatic moderation**, because the bot-platform rules ask a question the code could not answer: *what happens after a decision nobody made?* `/automod` deletes an invite and times out a flood **by itself**, and the only trace was a notice that deleted itself after ten/fifteen seconds — so a moderator asked "did the bot mute somebody?" had nothing to read, and the member it hit had no way to say it was wrong. Every automatic action now writes a card to the server's own moderation log through **one shared door** (`utils/modlog.py`: resolves `log_channel_id`, validates it, and reports the two refusals — nothing configured, a channel that no longer exists — as facts rather than exceptions), naming the rule that fired, the member, the channel and whether Discord actually accepted the action *(`timeout` can be refused and the filter then reads as if it worked — the card says **not muted** and how to fix it)*. The **`/appeal`** command is the other half: any member can file one review per five minutes, it lands as a real `Appeal` case in the log with their own words and their last three cases attached (and says out loud that nothing reverses automatically, and that a moderator answering it uses `/unmute` or `/unban`, which are logged like every other action), falling back to a DM to the server owner when no log channel is set — and the notices now end with the one sentence that names the door: *"If that was wrong, run `/appeal` — a human will read it."* **The type was the other real find, and it was everywhere**: the dashboard sheet carried **43 declarations between 10px and 11.5px** — the hint under the identity fields, the label on every metric, the server name in the rail — and the public sheet and two templates carried 18 more (`0.625rem`, `0.6875rem`, three `11px`), all under the 12px floor both reference sets state flatly. Every one is 12px now, the `.doc` measure came in from 76ch to 72ch and the console's Gaps list from 92ch to **72ch** (the laws' ceiling is 75 characters a line), three fields that only had a `placeholder` — the admin passkey, the per-server nickname and the per-server bio — have real labels, and the console hands `reducedMotion="user"` to its animation library, which is the only way a JS spring can hear a setting that a media query cannot reach. **The laws are now tests rather than a paragraph**, which is the point of the exercise: `tests/test_design_laws.py` grew a **floor under every font size** in all three sheets and every template (resolving `rem` against the root the sheets actually set, and saying in the docstring why `em` and `%` are deliberately not guessed at), a **measure check** (a line longer than 75 characters fails, and a sheet that declares no measure at all fails), a **name on every field** in both the templates and the JSX, and a **reduced-motion** check for the sheet *and* for the library hand-off. `tests/test_automod.py` is new and 15 of its tests are about the record rather than the filters: an invite deletion and a flood timeout each leave a card, a refused timeout is recorded as refused, an action with nowhere to log still acts and raises nothing, an appeal becomes a case, reaches the owner when there is no channel, tells the member when there is nowhere to send it, keeps its cooldown, and two source-reading guards fail if a future filter is added without an audit call or a notice that omits the appeal. **2,129 tests pass, 2 skipped** (30 in the design-laws file, 15 new here); `tsc` clean.
- **🪤 *The Architect's Legal Framework: Version 3.0* was handed over as a PDF, and it asked for something a footer link cannot be: three layers of anti-AI defence, a real clickwrap, and a Terms document that says who owns what — so the bot now refuses named crawlers on every host, answers a trap no person can reach, and asks a sign-in to press one of two buttons that are exactly the same size**: *"fix those stuff and compile and stuff according to its job."* The framework's own sentence is the reason none of this is a `robots.txt` edit — *"robots.txt is a request, and the collectors with the most to gain are the ones least likely to honour it"* — so the posture is three layers, and each exists for a way the one before it is ignored. **The directive**: `utils/ai_guard.py` builds `robots.txt` (each AI crawler — GPTBot, ClaudeBot, CCBot, Bytespider, PerplexityBot, Google-Extended, Applebot-Extended and 23 more — named with `Disallow: /` **before** the wildcard, because a rule written after `User-agent: *` is read by nobody) and `ai.txt` (the training prohibition in the convention crawlers look for, at `/ai.txt` and `/.well-known/ai.txt`), and **all four hosts now write those same two functions' output** — the app serves them, and `build_site`, `build_hub` and `build_owner` call them, where two of those hosts had published `User-agent: * / Allow: /` and nothing else, which is exactly how a policy ends up true on one host and absent on the next with nothing to show for it. **The reservation in headers**: `Content-Usage: train-ai=n`, `tdm-reservation: 1`, `tdm-policy` and `X-Robots-Tag: noai, noimageai` (plus `noindex, nofollow` on everything behind a sign-in) on every response from every surface, because a header reaches an API answer, an image and an error page as readily as it reaches HTML — and a static host has no Flask, so the `_headers` files carry them too. **The refusal**: a declared AI crawler is answered **403** with the addresses of the two documents it is choosing to ignore, on every path except the directives themselves (a crawler that cannot fetch the rule it is breaking has an argument, and refusing the policy would be an odd way to enforce it). **The trap**: two paths no page links a person to, disallowed in the directive, absent from every sitemap, `tabindex="-1"` and `aria-hidden` in every public footer, answered with a decoy that says what it is rather than a poisoned payload that invites an argument about who deceived whom — because the trap's value is that it cannot be argued with. A fetch is the only signal in this codebase that identifies a collector **positively** rather than guessing at one from behaviour, so it is recorded with the agent, the address, the country and a count (folded, because a collector that asks four hundred times should be one line with a count and not four hundred lines when somebody finally has to read it) in `net_guard_events` for 180 days, appears as counts on `/health` in its own `anti_ai` block, and reaches the operator as one card that names the path, the agent, the address and the two things that actually stop it: **a network block rather than a user-agent rule** (one costs the collector the proxies it rented, the other costs it one line), and the ledger row that a complaint is built on. The clause behind all of it is Terms §14, the policy is `AI_POLICY.md` (served at `/ai`, machine-readable at `/ai.txt`), and the operator's playbook — including the Cloudflare WAF expression to paste — is `docs/anti-ai.md`. **And the Terms became a clickwrap**, which is the half the framework is emphatic about: terms a person was never made to acknowledge are the terms most likely to be found unenforceable, and *a crawler cannot assent*, so a prohibition it never saw was never a term. Every door (the passkey form, the one-time link, Discord's callback, the owner console's form) now wraps its destination in `/agree`, which lists the three documents, shows the **revision** — the SHA-256 prefix of the files themselves, so a sentence added in September is not covered by an agreement made in June, and the dashboard asks again at the next sign-in — and offers two answers of exactly the same size: same class, same width, no pre-selection, no countdown, no confirmshaming, and a decline that signs the session out and records nothing. Accepting writes one row (revision, scope, address, browser, time) that the console's Access page can read; **a ledger that cannot be written still lets the person in and says so in words**, because the failure mode worth designing against is a clickwrap that quietly stops recording and keeps looking like it works. The policies themselves are never behind it — a policy you must accept before you may read is not a policy — and `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` / `GATED_PREFIXES` classify every route in the app, with a test that walks Flask's own route table and fails on a new route that was not classified. **The documents were rewritten to the framework's own clause list**, since the point of the exercise is legal rather than technical: the Terms gained the anti-AI clause, clickwrap and revision recording, output ownership with the similarity-of-content caveat, user warranties, third-party disclaimers, an explicit *no professional reliance* section with human oversight, **indemnity** with its procedural mechanics, **binding arbitration with a jury and class-action waiver and a 30-day opt-out**, notices and takedowns, a **grievance officer** with 7-day acknowledgement and 30-day resolution, export controls and sanctions, and a general section; the Privacy Policy gained the lawful-basis table, the rights section (GDPR, UK GDPR, CCPA/CPRA and the DPDP Act, with the grievance route), automated decisions and the human-review path (Terms §12), a cookies section that says out loud why there is no consent banner — one strictly-necessary cookie and nothing to consent to — security stated plainly without claiming encryption at rest, and transfers. **One real bug fell out of writing them**: the Terms now exceed Discord's 6,000-character embed ceiling, and `cogs/legal.py` added the *"the rest of it"* pointer **after** filling the card — so the card came to 6,053 characters, which Discord rejects outright, i.e. the failure was not a truncated policy but *no policy at all* in the one command a member runs to read it. The pointer is measured and reserved now, in both the character and the field budget, and the test asserts the ceiling holds *including* the field that says where the rest of it is. **2,128 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 72 new in `tests/test_ai_guard.py` and `tests/test_consent.py`, and the ones that matter are the negative ones: a browser, a monitor, `curl` and a reader with no `User-Agent` at all are still served, `GPTBotSimulator` is not OpenAI, the document that governs a crawler is still readable by it, every route is on one side of the public/gated split, the two answers on `/agree` are byte-identical in markup, declining records nothing, and an unwritable ledger leaves the person signed in with the page admitting what is missing. `tsc` clean; nothing in the console bundle changed.
- **🔐 A security pass over the changed surface found six things — two were real, two were only right because somebody typed a constant that way, and two were the scanner being unable to see a guard**: Snyk's SAST run over the working copy, with every finding traced rather than muted. **Real**: `find_free_port` probed a port by binding **every** interface (`0.0.0.0`) to answer a question about one, and it binds loopback now; `scripts/preview_cards.py` fed a **command-line argument straight into `exec`** — the one place in this project where a command line reaches code — so a revision is now validated by shape before git is asked for it (`--output=…`, quotes, whitespace and backticks are all refused) and the test asserts both halves, the checked name *and* the argument list that makes a shell metacharacter inert. **Guarded rather than fixed**: two URLs already pointed at the right host, and one of them carries a **bearer access token** while the other posts a **licence key somebody paid for** — so `discord_auth.checked_endpoint` and `plans.checked_endpoint` now refuse anything that is not HTTPS to `discord.com` / `api.lemonsqueezy.com`, called *before* the request is built and asserted to be on the path every outbound call takes, because "the endpoint is a constant" is a comment and a host check is a rule. The tests include the confusions that matter: `discord.com.evil.test`, `discordapp.com`, a userinfo host, a plaintext downgrade, and the loopback address. **Not real, and written down**: two DOM-XSS reports where the value reaching `<img src>` is a same-origin relative path produced by `iconSrc`, which refuses `//host/x`; React escapes it and assigns the attribute as a property, so the report is the scanner not *seeing* the guard — which is why `tests/test_outbound.py` now pins the guard, the funnel every request goes through, and that no page builds an icon URL by hand. 13 findings closed, 5 carried with their reasoning, and the one that matters most is the last: the scan is a starting point, not a verdict.
- **🎨 The owner console was rebuilt to a written set of design laws — and, more usefully, the laws are now *tests* rather than a paragraph somebody has to remember**: *"rebuild the whole /owner from the bottom: new UI, new UX, new rules"*, against the 60-30-10 palette rule, a single type scale, WCAG contrast, whitespace, consistent components, Fitts's and Hick's laws, keyboard navigation and immediate feedback. The type scale is the visible half: body text was **13px in components and 15px on the page** and is now 16px everywhere a sentence is read, with four smaller tiers for type that is *not* prose (an eyebrow, a table cell, a note under a figure) — the sheet had 74 hand-written font sizes in twelve values, including a 12.5px that existed on one element, and every one of them now resolves to a `--type-*` token. Contrast is measured rather than eyeballed: text is ≥ 4.5:1 against the surface it is drawn on and graphics ≥ 3:1, in **all four console themes** and in the public sheet's own palette, and the one token that cannot carry text (`--accent` on the public sheet, 4.01:1 by design) now has a test that fails if a rule ever paints type with it — the fix is always `--accent-text`, which is 8.94:1. Controls have a floor as a *token* (`--target-min: 44px`, `--tap-min` on the public sheet, Fitts's law): buttons, fields, rail links, palette rows and the theme swatches all read it, and the swatches went from a 22-pixel circle to a 44×44 target with the dot drawn inside it. Focus is one global `:focus-visible` ring, and an `outline: none` is only allowed where the same rule draws something else — which turned the two places the sheet had quietly removed one (the palette's input, the public forms) into a visible state instead of an absence. And **nine pages is past what a person scans**, so the rail is grouped — Fleet, Work, Owner, with the Overview above the headings — from `OWNER_GROUPS` on the server rather than a list in the React app, so a page nobody placed is drawn ungrouped rather than vanishing. `tests/test_design_laws.py` holds all of it: contrast per theme, the type floor, a console rule that invents a font size, one `h1` per page, `alt` on every image, the focus rules, the target floors, the nav arithmetic, and a scan that fails if a component writes a colour literal of its own — which is how the sheet's twelve stray `rgba(...)` values and one `#fff` became seven named neutral overlays.
