The Pages build answered exactly that — *"no such table: activity_messages"*, ``/ answered 500 while building index.html — the site would ship a broken page`` — and because the builder refuses to publish a page that answered 500, the deploy failed and the site stayed on the commit before it. **A marketing page must not be brought down by a table it only wanted a count from**, so the read now reports the module's own answer for it: a window with no schema to count in is **absent, not zero**, which is the rule `utils/figures.py` already states for a sleeping host at its extreme. **Both renderers were getting that wrong too, and only one of them was mine.** `_activity_tiles` and the page's own script each fell back to `"0"`, so an unread window was spelled as a *measurement* by whichever of the two painted last — **0 messages** under a card whose module spends a paragraph explaining why it never says that. Both fall back to the em dash the control figures already used. Verified the way the failure was found: the build command CI runs, run against a **clean clone made from `git ls-files`** — no `bot.db`, which is what Pages clones — now builds all four pages, and the workflow's own five post-build checks pass on its output (invite link, `_headers` with the CSP and `frame-ancestors`, the worker's two config files, the cat cursor, and no source tree in the 20 uploaded files). The front page renders with `—` for every figure it has not read and `11`/`100` for the two it can read from the feature registry, and the live `/api/figures` fills the same card in from the host that has a database. Three guards were watched to fail by mutation: the `OperationalError` catch narrowed to an exception that never fires, the server tile's fallback, and the script's. 1,551 tests pass (207 subtests), 2 skipped, 4 new. **Worth recording from the same deploy**: Render took `df1084c` on its own (`new_commit`, finished, previous deployment deactivated) and serves it — `/api/figures` answers 200 with 3 servers, 42 members and 467 messages this week, and all seven dashboard routes and all four console routes answer 302 rather than 404. **GitHub Actions is the thing that cannot publish right now**, and not for a code reason: both workflows' jobs print *"The job was not started because recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased"*, so `Site` never reaches its upload step and `Tests` never runs at all. The Pages project's own Git integration is the working path in the meantime, which is what this fix unblocks.
- **📄 Neither console is one long page any more, the site stops advertising what it is giving away, and the operator's console finally answers what the bot is *doing* rather than only what it is standing on**: four complaints that were one habit — a page answering a question about *everything* because splitting it was never worth a commit. **The dashboard is seven pages and the console is four.** `/dashboard` was 3,285 lines and the owner's console was one 761-line scroll, so "the composer" and "the status panel" were the same address, the rail was a scroll spy guessing which of nine sections the reader was looking at, and the palette's actions had to name a section that might not be on the page. Both consoles now have real pages (`DASHBOARD_PAGES`, `OWNER_PAGES`), each route renders one section, and the rail is seven links whose current page is `aria-current` — which the server sets and no script can disagree with. The panels were written as guarded IIFEs for exactly this, so the split is two conditions and three guards per page rather than a rewrite: the composer's listeners only register where a composer exists, and the scheduler's poll only starts where a queue does. A palette action that lives on another page now travels as a hash (`/dashboard/systems#btn-enable-all`) and the destination focuses it on arrival, so "Enable every system" from the Status page lands on the button instead of at the top of a page containing it. **The front page stopped describing a shop.** The landing page carried a **PLANS** band headed *"Everything is free today"* with a price-shaped card under it — leftover premium furniture on a product with `PLAN_CHECKOUT` off, and the one thing a visitor scrolling to the bottom of the front door should not find. It is gone, and what replaced the two preview cards' furniture is the deployment's own counts: `utils/figures.py` answers with **counts and never names** (a landing page may say *1,669 people reached*; it may not say which twelve — the per-channel and per-member breakdowns stay behind the dashboard's sign-in), reports **absent as absent rather than as zero** (a sleeping free-tier host has no gateway to ask, and a confident *0 servers* reads as a bot nobody uses), reads the tracker's tables synchronously on the cached path the rest of the web side already uses, and repeats the tracker's window arithmetic rather than importing it — with `tests/test_figures.py` comparing the two, because "last 7 days" has to mean the same seven days `/activity` and the weekly digest mean. **The console's Infrastructure page gained the half a third party cannot answer.** UptimeRobot's monitors say whether a host answers; nothing there knows whether anybody uses what it hosts. *What the bot is doing* is drawn from the same payload the landing page draws and `/api/figures` serves — servers, members, and the tracker's messages, voice, people and channels for **Today / Last 7 days / Last 30 days / All time** — server-rendered, so it says something true before its own script has run, with the window buttons switching between numbers already in the payload rather than asking for them. **And the box beside it was lying, which is what "fix the box" was about.** *This deployment* read **"Read when this page was rendered"** over three rows of which exactly one was ever re-read: the page polls `/health` every fifteen seconds for the gateway figure, and the site-publishing verdict and the configuration count sat beside it as old as the page load. All three rows are painted from that one poll now, the stamp says *re-read just now* rather than naming a time it did not read at, the first read happens on load rather than fifteen seconds later, and the dashboard's own copy of the card — which has no such poll — keeps its honest *"Read at render time"* wording instead of borrowing the console's. **Three defects came out of the split, two of them in the guards rather than the code.** `paintDeployment` re-stamped the card on an answer that carried neither block, claiming a read of rows it had not read — the same defect one layer down, a time that is true of the request and false of the readings under it found by running the painter in a real engine against a stubbed DOM rather than by reading it. A test that pinned the *identity page's* profile form was still asking `/dashboard` for it, which is the split working: it follows the form to `/dashboard/identity` now. And the selling-surface test that guarded the palette's dangling comma matched the entries the array happened to contain, so it broke the moment the array was restructured — it asserts the gate's **shape** instead (a selling gate inside a script opens where a statement opened and hands nothing to a shared list, and the array literal carries no conditional at all), which is the rule that actually keeps `}, ];` out of the page's script, and it was watched to fail by moving the boundary to a comma. 1,547 tests pass (194 subtests), 2 skipped, 19 of them new in `tests/test_figures.py`. **Considered and not done:** `scripts/build_site.py` still publishes `/pricing` while selling is off. Nothing links to it, no template offers it and its content is a free product's page that says paid plans may come later — which is what the operator asked to keep — but it is still a reachable orphan, and publishing it conditionally means threading a computed page list through `rewrite_url`, which is pure and heavily tested. Worth its own pass rather than a corner of this one.
- **🧾 "Commands in this server" was a promise about Discord's picker answered by our own database, so the panel now records what Discord actually came back with — and it stopped making you wait four seconds for a number it already had**: the panel is named for Discord's command list and says in its own copy that a command whose system is off is *"not in this server's picker at all"*. Both of its figures came from the local module cache, which is what the server **asked for**: ninety whether the republish had landed, was still behind its three-second settle, or had been refused outright by a spent daily write budget. The one place a refusal was visible at all was a line in the process ring buffer, behind a button on a different page, and the page's only gesture at any of it was a flat four-second timer that re-read the same local numbers — the wait changed nothing, which is why "I turn it off and it comes back after some time" had no answer on screen. **The missing half is `utils/command_publish.py`**: the publisher writes the outcome of every pass down and the page reads it, at the one choke point every publish already goes through (`_sync_scope`, so boot, both drains, the bulk publish and every module switch are covered by construction, plus the two paths that raise and never return a status at all). Four facts, each exactly true or honestly absent — whether Discord's copy was *read*, how many commands are **still to write**, when the pass finished, and Discord's own words when something went wrong — reported in four tones the panel colours with the dots the Status panel already uses. **The wording has one author.** This box is painted twice, by the server and by the page's own refresh, and the Status panel is the object lesson: two renderers that each spell the sentence is how the same box says two different things depending on which one painted it. So the template prints `command_scope_publish.text` and nothing else, and a test fails if the template ever starts composing the sentence from the numbers. **A count that is ambiguous is not printed.** A pass refused part-way through its writes cannot know what Discord holds — some of the writes added a command and some replaced one — so `held` is `None` there, because a figure under a heading is read as a measurement and an approximate measurement is worse than no measurement. **Reading this wrote a real bug into the publisher and then caught it.** `_sync_scope` has been reporting `registered = total - pending`, and `total` counts a *stale* command twice — once in what Discord already holds and again in the names to write — so the boot log's per-server count has been over by the number of changed commands since it was written. It is now `len(registered) - removed + len(missing)`, the three buckets being disjoint by construction. **And the four seconds are gone, replaced by a fact.** `/api/toggle` answers with the timestamp of the publish it supersedes and the panel asks again until that timestamp is *newer* — two readings of the server's own clock, never the browser's against the server's, because a host in another region would otherwise make the wait forever or give up early. A refused publish is a recorded outcome with a reason, so there is nothing left to wait out; a long wait now says so instead of leaving a line that still reads "checked four minutes ago" as if the click did nothing. **Three smaller ones came out of building it.** The ledger's first test run filed the publisher's records under `"<Guild id=… name=…>"` while the panel looked for an integer, so every server would have read as *never published* — the key unwraps a guild now, and the test that caught it pins a guild and its id to one record. The shell's own guard — *every id the page's script looks up must exist in the rendered page* — caught a third renderer immediately: the test helper that renders the page had not been given the reading, which is the same drift `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` warns about, so the helper builds it from the module rather than restating it and the harness records a real pass so its preview shows the line a deployment shows. And one existing assertion had to be made precise rather than weaker: `test_a_different_rulebook_reaches_the_page` forbade the string `2000` anywhere after the stylesheet as a proxy for "the template states no limit of its own", so it tripped over a two-second poll interval the moment one existed; it now looks in the limits declaration and at the markup attributes, which is what it always meant. Verified by driving the real panel in a browser through all four states and the whole refresh path: the server render carries the line before any script runs (green dot, "Discord holds 90 commands, matching what this server's switches say"), flipping a switch leaves the counts instant and then walks the line from *matching* to **"Discord still needs 3 commands written — 2 refused (a, b), and the next pass is in 45 seconds"** with a gold dot, only after a re-read answers with a newer stamp than the click, and all four tones paint the right colour with the panel keeping its last reading rather than blanking when an answer carries none. No console or page errors. Mutations watched to fail: the old count arithmetic, an unread scope recorded as agreement, the raise path left unwritten, and the ledger keyed by the object rather than its id. **Reading it back with fresh eyes found the clause that could never be right, and a page borrowing another scope's reading.** The one line `describe()` gained its `expected` argument to print — a count that disagrees with the switches — was composed as an opening with the disagreement *appended*, so it read **"Discord holds 87 commands, matching what this server's switches say. Discord holds 87 — 3 fewer than this server's switches say it should"**: it claimed agreement and named a disagreement in the same breath, and that is reachable on the first page load after any switch flip, because the panel's own figures move immediately while the last publish's do not. It is now one of three mutually exclusive shapes — agrees, disagrees, or no count to compare — so no sentence can say both. **And a page with no server selected was reading Discord's *shared* lane.** The route answers "no server" with the id `0` and renders the default switches, and the line mapped that sentinel onto `None`, which is Discord's global scope — the lane that holds the two DM-safe commands — so an unscoped page reported "Discord holds 2 — 2 more than this server's switches say it should" under a heading whose figures were the defaults, about a server it was not showing. The id is handed over as it is now, and a scope nobody has published has no reading, which is the honest answer. Both were driven through the **real route** before and after, and both guards watched to fail by mutation. 1,509 tests pass (1,511 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 2 more than the pass above.
- **🔎 The monitors panel read "Not connected" beside "read 0 seconds ago", because `at` is stamped by every *attempt* rather than by every reading**: the first thing the UptimeRobot integration did on a real deployment was contradict itself in the two lines a reader trusts most. `check()` sets `at` on all four of its outcomes — including the one where no key exists and **nothing is asked at all** — and `snapshot()` aged it unconditionally, so a panel with no data said a reading had been taken seconds ago while the box underneath said *"Not connected: set UPTIMEROBOT_API_KEY"*. Both sentences were true of different things, and the one that looks like a measurement is the one people believe. The age line now describes **the reading on screen**: with rows to age it counts them; with nothing to age it says `not connected`, or `no reading` when the API refused or could not be reached, instead of counting up from a clock the API never touched. **And the rows now carry their own timestamp.** `record()` keeps the last good readings through a failed refresh — deliberately, so a bad minute at UptimeRobot does not read as "you have no monitors" — but it kept them while stamping them with the time of the *attempt*, so a twenty-minute-old reading was called "read just now" in one line and "the last reading" in the next; `at` travels with the rows now. The sentence in the box uses the same worder as the line above it, because `taken 1200 seconds ago` beside `read 20 minutes ago` is one fact spelled twice — the exact defect the age line itself was fixed for a pass earlier, still living one line below it. `/health` stops publishing `checked_seconds_ago` when there is nothing to age, since an unauthenticated endpoint reporting "checked 0 seconds ago" on a deployment that has never asked is the same lie in a different place. **One more, found by driving the page rather than reading it:** the panel has two renderers — the server's and the script's — and the script appended a chip naming the setting to paste on top of a `detail` sentence that already names it, so pressing **Refresh** printed the variable a second time as a stray token after a full stop. The name belongs to the module that composes the sentence, so the template must not spell it, and a test says so. Verified by driving the real panel in a browser through all four states in one sitting — answering (2 rows, `read 1 minute ago`), the unconfigured payload (`0 rows`, `not connected`, no false claim), a return to answering, and a **Refresh** at each step — with no console errors, and by mutating each fix to watch its test fail: the rows losing their timestamp, the age line trusting `at` unconditionally, the detail sentence going back to raw seconds, and the script re-adding the chip. 1,476 tests pass (1,478 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 4 of them new.
- **💸 Nothing is for sale, so nothing links to the shop — on any of the six surfaces — and the page that explains that no longer quotes a price at you**: the gate added last week hid the tiers and the Buy buttons, and left every *link* standing. The dashboard's topbar and its command palette, the owner console, the landing page's nav in three places and its "See what you get" button, and the policy pages' nav and footer all pointed at `/pricing`, so the shop was one click away from everywhere and its entire content was that there is no shop. Worse, two of those links were in a worse state than visible: the pricing page's own free card still read **`₹0` beside a `/mo`** — a price tag *and* a billing period, on the one element whose job is to say the product is free. There is no number on it now, and no tier chip either; the band reads `Free · Everything included` and nothing else. **Gating the templates alone would have broken the other direction, and that is the half worth reading.** Jinja reads a missing name as false, so a route that forgets to pass the flag looks *perfectly correct* while the product is free and silently loses the link the day it is not — and three routes never passed it: the dashboard, the owner console and the policy pages. The flag reaches all five now, `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` passes it too (it renders two of those templates itself, and a harness that agrees about the payload and disagrees about a field is where every defect in that file has come from), and `tests/test_selling_surface.py` pins the two halves separately because they fail differently — the **templates** by rendering each one both ways, and the **wiring** by walking every `render_template(...)` call in `keep_alive.py` with a paren-matching reader and insisting the templates that show a topbar are never rendered without it. Four surfaces are driven through the real routes instead, with `plans.selling_enabled()` patched, and the reason that list is four and not six is recorded in the test: `/dashboard` and `/owner` need a session. Its own trap is recorded too — **the landing page answers a `curl` user agent with one health line** rather than the document, because that is what a monitor polls, so a check that does not send a browser's user agent inspects a 34-byte string and concludes the links are gone. Verified by switching the real environment variable both ways and reading all six pages: with selling off, **zero** occurrences of `/pricing`, of `₹`, and of `/mo` anywhere; with `PLAN_CHECKOUT=1`, every link back where it belongs — 4 on the landing page (menu nav, main nav, CTA, footer), 1 on the dashboard *and* 1 in its palette, 1 on the console, 1 on the pricing page's own nav, 2 each on the policies — plus the three tier prices and their periods. Each guard was watched to fail by mutation: the owner route's flag removed, one link un-gated, and the free card's price restored. **And the channel picker from your screenshot was white rectangles.** *"Fix the UI/UX"* showed rows painted `#fff` with a black `✓` and no channel name, and the cause was that each row is a **`<button>`** — the right element for a thing you press — which arrives from the browser with its own light background and its own black text, and `.picker-row` had never turned either off. Nothing about `display: flex` does that, and it is the same defect in `.mention-result`, so the tag picker was blank too; the `✓` was the only thing painted in a colour the browser did not supply, which is what made it read as a broken stylesheet rather than as two overlapping backgrounds. Both now unset the native control by hand — `background`, `color`, `font`, `border` *and* **`appearance`**, since `background` alone is enough in Chromium and not on Safari. Measured in a browser under the real sheets rather than eyeballed: both rows compute `appearance: none`, the row background is transparent so the card's `rgb(23,23,26)` shows through, the selected row takes the accent tint, and the channel name is `rgb(244,242,239)` on that surface — a contrast of **8.76:1** where it had been near-white on white. 1,472 tests pass (1,474 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 8 of them new.
- **🔐 The dashboard had two doors into one room and the looser one decided, so a server's moderators could sign in to it — and every picture it showed was blocked by its own security policy**: two reports, one afternoon, and the second turned out to explain weeks of "the logo is still broken". **The permission bar was stated twice and enforced at two different heights.** A one-time link is only minted for somebody with **Administrator**, but `Sign in with Discord` accepted **Manage Server** as well (`ADMIN_BITS = MANAGE_GUILD | ADMINISTRATOR`), and Manage Server is the bit Discord hands to moderators — servers routinely grant it to a whole staff role. So anyone on that role could open a server's dashboard and reach every one of its eleven system switches, its command list, and the bot's name, picture and bio *in that server*, while the link beside it refused exactly those people. The bar is `ADMINISTRATOR` (or being the owner) now, so both ways in ask the same thing, and a test pins them together so whoever loosens one has to loosen the other in the same commit and will read the failure while doing it. **The images were a single defect seen from four places.** Our own `Content-Security-Policy` sets `img-src 'self' data:` — worth keeping, it is what stops a page whose template was tampered with from dialling out — and every template was handing the browser a `cdn.discordapp.com` URL: the server icons on `/servers`, the signed-in person's avatar in the header, and the bot's own avatar fallback, which is why the fix made one pass earlier found the avatar *correctly* and still rendered a broken image. Uploaded pictures were the only ones that worked, and only because `/api/identity/image` already served them from here. **The fix is origin-side rather than a wider policy**: a new `/api/image` answers for exactly three pictures — the bot's avatar, the signed-in person's, and a server icon the session may already see — and `discord_auth.fetch_image` re-checks the host even though every address is built here from a cached Discord object, so the worst a crafted parameter can reach is a Discord picture. Its 404 is a *normal* answer: the row draws the server's initial underneath and the picture over it, so a picture that never arrives uncovers the letter instead of the browser's broken-image glyph. `discord_auth.icon_url` is deleted with it — dead, and it built precisely the address the policy blocks. **A second console, for the seat that was never served.** `/dashboard` answers *what is this one server doing*, one server at a time, on purpose; the operator's question is **which** of them is the one that is wrong, and answering it there meant opening them one at a time and remembering. `/owner` is that answer: every server in one list beside the numbers that make a bad one obvious — members, systems on, and the names of the ones that are off — with the infrastructure underneath, reachable by the passkey and by nothing else. A scoped link and a Discord sign-in are redirected out of it rather than shown a reduced copy, and the tests assert on what was *served* (no server name, id or count comes back) rather than on a status code, because a 302 in front of a rendered fleet would pass the weaker check. **It is interactive, and so is the front page.** The console filters and orders its servers locally — `/` focuses the filter, as on every list page people already use — counts its figures up on first sight, re-reads the gateway every fifteen seconds **only while the tab is visible**, and keeps this process's own log behind a button. The landing page gained the one claim a reader cannot check by reading: *a system switched off leaves that server's picker and nowhere else* now ships as something to operate — press any of the eleven systems and watch the command list change, built from the same registry the cards above come from so it cannot drift from them or invent a system. It renders **with everything on** before any script runs, which is the truth about a server nobody has configured. **Three defects came out of rendering the pages rather than reading them.** The console's search box gave `.search-icon` to an inline `<svg>` — a class written for a text glyph (`🔍`), which carries no width, height or fill, so the icon drew as a **173 px black disc** across the page; sized and stroked for an SVG now, with the rule pinned so deleting it fails a test. And the landing page had been printing **`//rank`**, `//balance` and `//daily` **on the live site**: every name in the registry already carries its leading slash, and the template added its own, which the dashboard's module cards have never done — so the two surfaces disagreed and only one of them was wrong. The payload that feeds the new panel is serialised in Python and marked `|safe` on purpose: Jinja's autoescaping turned every quote into `&#34;`, which is valid HTML and invalid JSON, so the panel fell back to its static state on every page while looking entirely correct in the markup. 1,464 tests pass (1,466 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 54 of them new.
- **🛠️ The dashboard's message composer was dead, and nothing on the server, in the render or in the suite said so**: driving the rebuilt page found it in the first minute — tick a channel and the counter says **"1 of 5 selected"** while the reason under the box still reads **"Pick at least one channel."** and the Send button stays disabled, with a `TypeError` in the browser console. The cause was one id used twice: the composer's section heading was `<h2 id="composer-title">` and the embed-title *input* was `id="composer-title"` too, so `getElementById` handed the script the **heading** — an element with no `.value` — and every call to the composer's rulebook died on `.value.trim()`. The button can therefore never enable, which makes the dashboard's headline feature unusable, and the failure is invisible from every direction this project normally checks: the page renders, the markup is valid HTML, the endpoint is fine, and all 1,395 tests passed. The heading has its own id now (the input keeps the one its `<label for>` and the script both need), and **the class is guarded rather than the instance**: `tests/test_shell.py` fails if any template declares the same id twice, if an id the page's script looks up is missing from the rendered page, or if an id it reads as a form control lands on something that is not one — 65 looked-up ids, all four mutations watched to fail. **Three more defects came out of the same pass.** A double-clicked module switch sent **one** request and kept the second click's flip, leaving `checked=true` beside `aria-checked="false"` and a label reading "Disabled" — the page contradicting itself, and the next reload silently undoing the switch; the pending guard now puts the switch back to the state actually in flight and says which. The **schedule time was not part of the composer's rulebook**, so a time in the past left an enabled button directly under a field reading "that is in the past", and the field never re-asked the gate at all; both are fixed and the hint and the refusal now agree. And the palette's trigger **had no accessible name below 860px**, where the stylesheet hides both its wording and its key hint — a 39px magnifier an assistive reader cannot name. Two smaller truths came with them: the key hint said `Ctrl` on a machine whose handler accepts `⌘` (the operator's), and the status panel's age line read **"read 1 minutes ago"**. **The preview harness got three of its own**, which is where every defect in that file has come from — a hand-written stub that agrees with the real endpoint about the shape and disagrees about a field. Its `/api/uptime` never sent `age_text`, so the panel's "read N seconds ago" line sat on "not read yet in this process" through every Refresh while the harness's own comment claimed it moved; its invite links carried a placeholder application id while `render.yaml` commits the real **public** one, so "Add to Discord" went to an application Discord does not have; and `/pricing` and `/health` answered Flask's 404 although the dashboard's topbar links to Pricing. Verified the way the defects were found: the composer now sends (one POST for three rapid clicks, receipt "Sent", body cleared), a double-click leaves all four switch signals agreeing and the `10 / 11` metric with them, a past time disables the button, a scheduled message queues and cancels, a draft survives a reload with its channels, and the palette opens on `Ctrl`/`⌘`+`K`, filters, runs an action and hands focus back. 1,410 tests pass (1,412 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped.
- **🖥️ The control panel was rebuilt around a command palette and one stylesheet, the pointer became a cat that can never become the *only* cursor, and the deploy finally asks UptimeRobot what it has seen**: three things the page had outgrown at once. **The layout left the template.** `templates/dashboard.html` carried 1,660 lines of CSS inline, which made it the third-largest file in the repository and meant a change to one card was a change to a page; the shell — the rail, the topbar, every card, every control, the palette and the toasts — is now `static/dashboard.css`, and the template keeps the two rules that genuinely belong to the *document* rather than to the sheet (its own palette-open state and its own paper). **Reaching anything no longer means scrolling three thousand pixels of module cards**: the topbar's *Jump to anything…* opens a command palette on `Ctrl`/`Cmd`+`K` that indexes the seven sections, all eleven systems by their own names and their commands, and the actions that are otherwise a scroll and a click away — filtered as you type, arrow-navigable, `Enter` opens, `Esc` closes and returns focus to the button that opened it, with a **"Nothing matches that."** line because a dialog that goes blank on a typo reads as a broken dialog. Verified live rather than read: the palette opens with focus already in its input, `econ` narrows to two systems, a junk string shows the empty state, and `Escape` puts focus back on the trigger. **The custom cursor is a cat now, and the interesting half of that is the failure it is built to avoid.** A dot tracks the pointer exactly and a ring chases it with weight, a click prints a paw, and a dim accent light drifts behind the content — additive, pointer-driven, and the loop stops itself as soon as the ring has caught up, so an idle page costs nothing (the same budget the deleted paw-print canvas was removed for). The native cursor is hidden by **`html.has-meow-cursor`** — a class the script adds *after* the layer is in the document, on a fine pointer, with motion wanted — because `cursor: none` on a page whose script then fails is a page with **no pointer at all**, and the sheet's own comments say so. Both cat SVGs are the base layer underneath it, so a touch device and a reduced-motion visitor get the cat as their real cursor while the layer draws for nobody. `tests/test_shell.py` (15 new) pins the properties rather than the pixels, and its CSS reader was **rewritten after it passed for the wrong reason**: the first version split the sheet on `}`, which handed the first rule inside each `@media` a "selector" that was the whole file up to that point, so a *bare* `cursor: none` would have passed by picking up the word `has-meow-cursor` from a rule hundreds of lines earlier. It walks the braces now. Four mutations were watched to fail — a bare `cursor: none`, the palette's dialog role removed, a rail anchor pointing at a section that does not exist, and the layout moving back into the template. **And the deploy can now answer *has this been reliable lately*, not only *is it up now*.** `utils/uptime_status.py` reads UptimeRobot's read-only API — the same monitors already watching this host — through `getMonitors`, which returns the state and the 1/7/30-day ratios in one response. The rows are rendered in the dashboard's status panel and served by `/api/uptime` behind the sign-in, while **`/health` carries counts and one ratio and no names**, on the same rule `config` and `site_guard` already follow. Nothing is fetched while a page is being answered: a background loop on the bot's own event loop refreshes a snapshot and every surface reads that, so an open dashboard costs UptimeRobot nothing. Absent configuration is a *state* — with no key the panel names the variable instead of showing an empty list, because an empty list reads as "you have no monitors" and that is a very different claim from "the integration is off" — and a refresh that could not ask keeps the last rows, marked stale. **The change found two defects of its own, both by being driven live.** The panel rendered the raw `at` timestamp as an age, so it said **"read 1789848173 seconds ago"**; age is a fact about *now* and is worded where it is read (`age_text`). And `headline()` — which decides whether a change is worth a card in `#bot-error` — re-reported the same transition to every caller, so a manual refresh beside the loop's own pass would have sent the same alert twice; it now *spends* the transition and says it exactly once. The test that pinned the first of those was itself wrong and was fixed rather than the code: it asserted the epoch appeared nowhere on the page, and the page legitimately carries the current epoch as `SERVER_NOW`, the clock-skew seed "in 2 hours" depends on — the assertion now names the sentence (`read <at> seconds ago`) instead. **UptimeRobot is declared where it has to be to exist**: `UPTIMEROBOT_API_KEY` and `UPTIMEROBOT_STATUS_PAGE` are in `render.yaml` as prompts and in `.env.example` and `docs/deployment.md` with what their absence costs, because a setting the code reads and the deploy never offers is a feature that is simply off on the host with no error to show for it — the omission this manifest has been caught by three times. 1,403 tests pass (1,405 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 41 of them new.
- **📡 Something now watches the public site from inside the bot, because the failure it watches for has no symptom anywhere else — and its first live run proved the point by reporting a leak that was not there**: when the Pages project spent hours publishing this private repository, every page on the site looked correct, the bot stayed connected, and the deploy reported **success**; the one signal was a line in a build log nobody reads. Fixing the settings could not cover the part that mattered — **nothing would have noticed a second time**. `utils/site_guard.py` asks the site for nine paths that exist in this checkout (a root script, a package module, a feature file, a config, a dotfile, the README and a test) and says so when one of them answers. It runs **on a timer in the bot process, not in CI**, for the reason this project had just lived through: at the time it was written, the `Tests` and `Site` workflows had been failing for an hour and a half because the account's Actions jobs would not start at all — *"The job was not started because recent account payments have failed"* — so nothing in them ran, and a guard against a publishing mistake that can be switched off by a payment failure is not a guard. This process is always up and is already in the operator's server, so the alarm reaches a person with no new service, token or dashboard. **The first live run reported the exposure as still open, and it was wrong** — the most useful thing this change produced. The deployment was 14 files and clean, while seven paths answered `200` with 71 KB of real source, `cf-cache-status: HIT`, `age: 2073`: copies of the bad deploy still being handed out at the bare URL half an hour after the origin had begun refusing it. Pages keeps a previous deployment's assets served for a while so a rollout cannot break an app mid-visit, and **re-deploying does not clear it** — three deployments followed and the copies stayed, with `age` climbing past 2500s while the stored response carried `max-age=300, must-revalidate`, and a client `Cache-Control: no-cache` did not force revalidation either. So the guard asks with **a unique query string**, which is a different URL to a cache and therefore asks the deployment; a `GET`, never the cheaper `HEAD`, because a host answering `405` to `HEAD` would read as *not exposed*, which is the one answer this must never give; and it asks the bare URL **only after** the deployment has refused a path, so a stored copy is found without inverting what its answer means. Verified that cache-busting does not cost the check its teeth: `/`, `/static/meow.css`, `/pricing` and `/login` still answer `200` and `/dashboard` still answers `302` with a query string attached. **Four verdicts, because the remedies differ and only one of them is the build**: `exposed` (the deployment is publishing the tree — fix the build settings, reported every cycle), `cached` (a stored copy still answers while the deployment refuses it — the build settings are correct and a redeploy will not help; the card says *not this*, which is the whole reason the near-miss above is a near-miss), `unreachable` (nothing was established, because *no leak found* and *no answer* are different claims and only one of them is reassuring), and a fifth that came out of thinking about how this reads wrong: **a control probe** for a path that does not exist, since a host that answers everything makes a `200` on `/bot.py` say nothing about `/bot.py` — that verdict is `fallback` and the card says the site cannot be checked this way rather than calling it a leak. `/health` carries the state as `site_guard` with counts and **no names**, on the same reasoning as `config`: that endpoint is unauthenticated, and the alert that names paths goes to the operator's channel. It is deliberately not folded into the top-level `status`, because the bot being healthy and the site being wrongly configured are separate claims. `SITE_GUARD_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (six hours) and `SITE_GUARD_FIRST_CHECK_SECONDS` (ninety, so a deploy is re-checked shortly after boot) tune it. **The check was verified by breaking it**: four mutations of the module each watched to fail — removing the cache-buster, removing the control probe, removing the throttle, and treating a cached copy as an exposure — costing 1, 2, 1 and 7 tests respectively, so no line of it is decorative. Two more were aimed at `docs/deployment.md`, whose own hand-check would have read the cache, and at documenting a path the check does not watch; both were caught. Driven end to end against the live host, where it reports what is actually true today: `cached`, seven paths, the deployment not the problem. 1,362 tests pass (1,364 under `unittest` discovery), 37 of them new.
- **🚨 The site at `meow-bot.pages.dev` was publishing the private repository, and the same misconfiguration is why `/dashboard` and `/login` answered 404 there**: the Pages project had a Git integration pointed at this repository and **no build command and no output directory**, and a static host handed no instructions publishes the directory it is given — so it published the checkout. Its own log said so in plain words: *No build command specified. Skipping build step.*, then *Uploading... (185/185)*, and this repository has exactly **185 tracked files**. While that lasted, `/bot.py`, `/utils/cloud_sync.py`, `/cogs/economy.py`, `/render.yaml`, `/Procfile` and `/.env.example` all answered **200** at the project's public address — the whole tree, readable by anyone, with `README.md` as an index. **No secret value was exposed**, and that is a property of where secrets live rather than luck: every credential is read from the host's environment and none is committed, which is exactly the discipline that made this survivable. The same missing build command explains the second symptom, which was reported separately as several `404`s: `_worker.js` and `_routes.json` are written *by the build*, and with no build there was nothing to reverse-proxy `/login`, `/dashboard`, `/servers`, `/api`, `/market` and `/health` to Render — so the dashboard and the sign-in page 404'd on the Pages domain while working on Render, and the two hosts disagreed about what exists. **The fix is the two settings that were empty**: the build command now runs the same thing CI does (`pip install -r requirements.txt && python scripts/build_site.py --out _site --proxy`) with the output directory `_site`, and the retried deployment uploads **14 files instead of 185** — the four pages, their assets, and the worker's two config files. Verified by probing production rather than by reading the config: eleven source paths answer **404** (including the ones that answered 200 an hour earlier), all four pages and the stylesheet still answer **200**, and `/login` now serves the real sign-in page (`<title>Sign in — Meow</title>`) while `/dashboard` and `/servers` answer **302 → /login**, which is the correct answer for a signed-out visitor. `scripts/build_site.py` refuses to write into the repository root now — the guard is a `SystemExit`, not a warning, because by the time the upload runs it is too late to take it back — and it is pinned four ways (the root spelled four ways, plus a real build into an ordinary directory so the guard cannot become a trap), each watched to fail by removing the check. The whole configuration is written down in `docs/deployment.md`, including the two commands that tell a correct deployment from a broken one, because the setting that caused this is invisible from the repository and one dashboard edit away from coming back. 1,325 tests pass (1,327 under `unittest` discovery), 2 of them new.
- **🧹 Two things on the dashboard were drawn by something other than the dashboard, and both are gone now — the image pickers and a full-page canvas of drifting paw prints**: the identity panel's two image controls were the browser's own `<input type="file">`, so "Choose file" and "No file chosen" arrived in the operating system's typeface and its own colors, which CSS cannot style at all — which made the two controls an administrator uses to set the bot's picture and banner the one place on the page where the product stopped and macOS started, sitting immediately above a Save button this page draws for itself. Each is a styled `<label for>` beside the input now, with the chosen filename in the page's own mono face. The input itself is still in the DOM and still focusable — visually hidden rather than `display:none`, because a display-none input cannot be reached by keyboard and the whole control would then be mouse-only — so the save path reads exactly the `files` it read before, and the code that clears the inputs after a save fires the same `change` event, so a successful upload cannot leave a stale filename sitting on screen next to an input that no longer holds a file. **`static/particles.js` is deleted**, along with the `<script>` tag that loaded it on five pages: the dashboard, the market, the sign-in page, the policies and the server picker. It painted a fixed, full-viewport canvas of 45 drifting dots and paw prints from a `requestAnimationFrame` loop that redrew every shape every frame for the entire life of the page, on a control panel that people leave open in a tab. It carried no information, and the two pages designed after it never reached for it, which is the tell rather than the argument. `tests/test_shared_system.py` now pins the absence instead of the behaviour, because removing a decoration is not a thing a test can notice: the file and every reference to it are asserted gone, so putting the canvas back has to be deliberate. 1,323 tests pass (1,325 under `unittest` discovery), 2 of them new.
- **🩺 Nothing in this deployment knew what it was missing, so a feature that was configured-and-off looked exactly like one that was broken — there is one place that answers it now, and finding it turned up a bug that made every `.env` setting invisible to the half of the app that reads the environment at import**: forty-odd `os.getenv` calls were spread across the tree, each with its own fallback and its own private decision about whether to say anything when it came up empty — three printed a warning, the rest said nothing — and nothing anywhere held the whole set. The only way to answer "is this deployment configured?" was for a person to read two files and the host's dashboard and hold them in their head. That gap has now cost this project three real incidents: `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` declared in `render.yaml` and absent from the running service, so the live site rendered one button fewer and nothing said why; a deploy that skipped publishing for four commits while every run reported **success**; and, found while writing this, **`load_dotenv()` called *after* `keep_alive` and `utils` were imported** — their module-level reads had already happened, so a `.env`, which is the documented way to configure this locally, reached `DISCORD_TOKEN` and nothing else: the dashboard ran on a throwaway session key with passkey sign-in switched off and backups disabled, on a machine where all three were filled in. `utils/config_audit.py` is now one declared set — every setting, what it turns on, what its absence *costs*, and whether it is a credential — with three consumers on surfaces people already use: the **boot log** opens with what is off and what that costs, before anything connects; **`/health`** gains a `config` block that counts and deliberately does not name, because it is unauthenticated and "this deployment has no sign-in secret" is not a sentence to hand a stranger; and **`/api/config`** carries the names and the costs behind the administrator's sign-in, where a one-time link counts as an administrator on purpose — *why the operator cannot get in the other way* is the most useful thing it has to say, and `ADMIN_PASSKEY` being the missing setting is exactly that case, so requiring the passkey would hide the answer behind the question. It reports presence and never a value, for every setting, secret or not, so the property does not depend on the route being guarded correctly. **The list is checked against the code rather than trusted**: two tests walk the tree for every way it reaches for the environment — including the two indirections a plain `getenv` search misses, a name handed to `_env_flag(…)` and the names held as values inside the `*_ENV` mappings — and fail in both directions. That caught a real omission in this registry's own first draft (`DISCORD_CLIENT_ID`, the variable the whole incident turned on), five settings whose absence turns something off but which `.env.example` documented nowhere (the update channel and role, the deploy hook, the owner id, and `DASHBOARD_HTTPS`), and a guard that passed on a *mention*: deleting `- key: FLASK_SECRET_KEY` from `render.yaml` still satisfied the first version, because the paragraph above that entry explains the omission and names the variable in prose. All three are fixed and each was watched to fail by mutation, which is how the third was found at all. **Two absences are deliberately not reported**, and the reasoning is the same in both cases — a report that cries wolf every boot is a report nobody reads by the third deploy, which is the failure this exists to prevent: `DASHBOARD_HTTPS` and `TRUST_PROXY_HEADERS` are *inferred* from `RENDER`/`RAILWAY_ENVIRONMENT`, so on Render they are absent and correct at the same time, and they are reported only where nothing supplies them; `MARKET_ROLE_ID` is a retired spelling of `MARKET_ROLE_IDS`, so one of the two is always unset and flagging it would flag every deployment that configured the gate correctly. Verified through the real surface rather than from the code: a genuine `python bot.py` boot prints the block and then the warnings that already existed, `/health` names no setting and still answers `status: ok` so no monitor changes behaviour, and `/api/config` answers **401** unauthenticated, **403** for a guild-scoped session, and **200** with the full table for the operator — with the request driven against a sentinel value planted in every variable to prove none of them is echoed. 1,321 tests pass (1,323 under `unittest` discovery), 18 of them new.
- **🧭 The deploy's success line announced a hostname that no longer exists, and the four places the Pages project is named are now compared to each other**: the project was renamed `meow-bot-site` → `meow-bot`, and the commit that renamed it updated the API calls and the `--project-name` with it — but not the last line of the run, which printed `Published <sha> to https://meow-bot-site.pages.dev`. That host now answers **530, project not found** (measured, not assumed: `wrangler pages deployment list --project-name meow-bot-site` refuses it *Project not found*, while the `meow-bot` project is serving `86eaa55`), so the one line a person reads to decide whether a deploy worked pointed at a URL that does not exist. It is the shape a rename leaves behind: every functional reference moves in the commit that renames the thing, and the *message about* the reference is the one nothing compiles or calls. `tests/test_site_workflow.py` no longer compares each mention to a literal but compares the four to each other — every `pages/projects/<name>` the preflight and the landed check ask, the project named to wrangler, and the URL printed on success have to be one name — and the assertion was watched to fail by putting the dead host back.
- **🎨 The dashboard, the market and the front page are one product now, and the reason they were not was not the one anybody would guess**: it was not motion, and it was not the layout. `templates/dashboard.html` re-declared **every shared token under the same name with a different value** — its `--bg` was the blue-black that `static/meow.css` documents as the thing it moved away from, its accent was Discord blurple beside the shared indigo, its radii disagreed by two pixels — so a page linking the shared sheet and this one painted two palettes at once, and neither was visible as a bug. Underneath that sat a *literal* layer duplicating the same roles a third time (`#4ade80`, `#ff6b6b`, `#facc15`, `rgba(88,101,242,…)`), and `templates/market_live.html` carried its own `:root` whose names — `--bg`, `--accent` — are the shared sheet's own, so it was repainting rules it only meant to read. The shared sheet had leftovers of the same kind: the aurora's grid was slate and the primary button's glow was blurple, a stale hue inside the file that owns the palette. There is one palette now, every `var()` on every page resolves, and the market's block is *aliases* (`--panel: var(--surface-card)`) rather than copies — an unresolved `var()` is an empty value, which is exactly how this stayed invisible. **Motion arrives on `motion.js` with the amount decided per page**: the dashboard gets the same primitives and **no curtain**, because a landing page's job is to arrive and a control panel's is to already be there — a progress bar in front of a switch somebody came to flip is a cost paid every visit for one impression. Reveals are 10px rather than 28px and stagger only across the first screen; everything below reveals on scroll. **The reveal primitive itself had a defect that only the dashboard could expose**: it left `opacity` and `transform` inline forever, and an inline style outranks every CSS rule for the life of the page, so a revealed `.module-card` could never dim when `.busy` was set and never responded to a hover — a reveal removing behaviours it had never touched, correct in each file and wrong in the pair. It now hands the element back to its stylesheet when it settles, on the reduced-motion path too, which is the path that exists precisely so the page can be still. Every remaining hand-written `?v=` string went to `asset_version()` with it — the favicon in seven templates and `particles.js` in five — the same defect class as the stylesheet the previous commit fixed, and the dashboard was the last place carrying them. `tests/test_shared_system.py` pins the rules rather than the hex, because every one of them failed silently: a template adding `:root { --bg: … }` looks like a reasonable local choice and nothing on screen says otherwise.
- **🆓 Nothing is for sale, so nothing is advertised — and the invite asks for `Administrator` again, beside a second link for owners who will not grant it**: two decisions this repository had argued the other way, each revisited on the operator's call. **The premium surface is gone while `PLAN_CHECKOUT` is off.** The page kept describing three tiers — prices in Indian rupees, feature lists, a "Most Popular" badge, and a comparison table with columns for plans nobody could buy — with only the *buttons* gated by the switch, so a visitor who opened it to ask "what does this cost me" was answered with a shop that is not open. With selling off it is now a free product's page: one plan, every system from the registry the bot actually loads (including the five this page used to sell), the real price from `utils/plans.py`, and a note that paid plans may come later — worded so it cannot promise what `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` could take back, which is what `test_the_free_page_says_what_happens_later_without_promising_the_world` pins from both sides. The tiers, prices and Buy buttons all return together when `PLAN_CHECKOUT=1`: one switch, two whole pages, and no in-between state where a price is listed and cannot be paid. The FAQ moved with it — answers about payment methods and cancellation are about a purchase nobody can make, so they appear only where there is one, and the free state answers the questions a visitor actually has. `/bot plan` follows its own advice: with nothing on sale it no longer tells an administrator to activate a key they cannot buy. **The invite asks for `Administrator` again**, and the argument that removed it is still the honest one — a public invite is the page's real job, and Administrator is the first thing a careful owner reads before closing the tab — but the operator weighed it against the cost the other way round: the explicit list is *always* one permission behind the code, and every feature added after it was written reaches somebody's server as `Missing Permissions`. So the answer is two links, not one, offered together wherever a person decides to add the bot (the landing page's step card and plans band, the pricing page's free card, `/servers`), built by one helper so a page that offers the Administrator link can never fail to offer the other. Both are pinned: the default carries Administrator and **nothing else** — checked against `discord.Permissions(administrator=True).value`, so a second bit cannot ride along — and the limited one must never grow it, asserted against the sum of the bits as well as the names, because a bit quietly *added* to that list keeps every name-based assertion passing. The site tests decode the real `permissions=` parameter out of both links on two pages, which is where the one harness trap lives: Jinja escapes the `&` between query parameters, so the markup has to be read back as `&amp;` before the query is parsed or every key comes out as `amp;permissions`. README's setup steps no longer describe one grant, `docs/architecture.md` answers for both links and both pricing states, and 1,301 tests pass (1,299 under pytest, 2 skipped) — 7 new, and three that used to assert the old behaviour were rewritten rather than deleted: the pricing page's "the tiers stay, this is 'not on sale yet'" test now asserts the opposite, because that was the defect.
- **🚫 A token that can read a Pages project is not a token that can publish to it, and the preflight now asks the second question**: the first real run of the deploy workflow got past the configuration check — which is what the commit below made visible — and then died inside wrangler with `Authentication error [code: 10000]` on `POST /accounts/…/pages/projects/meow-bot-site/upload-token`. Measured through the API rather than guessed: the deployed token answers **200** to the project GET and **403** to that POST, and its own policy list holds 241 permission groups including `Pages Read` with neither `Pages Write` nor the dashboard name for it, *Cloudflare Pages: Edit*. Every read in the tree works, and publishing was the one thing it could not do — which "is this token valid" and "can it read the project" both answer yes to. So the check is the capability the deploy actually needs: a Pages **upload token**, which creates nothing, publishes nothing and mints only the short-lived credential the upload would use, so it exercises no authority the next step does not already have. A refusal now names the permission, the API's name for it, the dashboard's name for it, and the command that replaces the secret, instead of a stack trace from inside a third-party action. Two smaller things in the same step: a missing `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID` is refused *before* any call at all, where it used to pass whenever the token endpoint answered 200 and then fail in wrangler with the project unnamed; and acceptance no longer keys off the read status, which a read-only token passes — pinned as absent, because that is the exact mistake. All four paths were executed against the live API: the real token is caught with *Cloudflare Pages: Edit* named, a garbage token reports both statuses, a missing account id fails by name, and no token still warns and skips green. The success path cannot be run from here, because it needs a token that has the permission, so the next run after the secret is replaced is what demonstrates it.
- **🚦 A green workflow no longer means a published site, and the deploy now checks that it landed**: `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID` was set and `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` was not, and the site spent four commits in that state while every `Site` run reported **success** — `meow-bot-site.pages.dev` was serving `0af327e`, from before all four of them. Nothing was broken except the signal: every build guard was about the build, which was fine, and the one thing that reached a person was a `::notice`, which does not appear on the checks list — so `success` was true of the build and false of the deploy, with nowhere to notice the difference. The missing-token path now emits a **warning**, which is on the checks list, and writes a job summary headed *Published: no* naming the secret and the command that sets it. A token that is set but rejected fails **before** wrangler starts, with the status the API answered, because an expired token otherwise surfaces as an opaque 401 from inside a third-party action. And an accepted upload is a different claim from a published site: the deploy job downloads an artifact and never checks out the repository, so `wrangler pages deploy` attached no commit at all and the deployment was unattributable — it now passes `--commit-hash` explicitly, with a fixed message because a head commit subject would reach a shell in a job that holds a token, and a final step asks Cloudflare which commit production serves and fails if it is not this one. `tests/test_site_workflow.py` pins the rule with a dependency-free read of the YAML, and the central assertion was watched to fail by putting the notice back. One trap is recorded in the workflow itself: the reader is a **single line** on purpose, because `python3 -c` over a multi-line block scalar fails on the indent as an unexpected indent on its first statement — a YAML parse error in a workflow whose only symptom is that it never runs. **One thing only running it could find**: the token endpoint answers **401** for an *account-owned* token, and a Pages-scoped token usually is one — the token this deployment now uses returns `401 Invalid API Token` there while the Pages API answers 200 and deploys with it, so a preflight that insisted on the token endpoint would have red-failed a good token *before* wrangler ran, which is worse than the silence it replaced. It asks the account's Pages project as well now, and only both failing is a refusal; a missing account id gets a sentence of its own instead of a confusing 404.
- **🔒 The premium tiers stay, and nothing can be bought until an operator says so: selling is now a switch, not a consequence of having a checkout link**: two URLs pasted into a host during an evening of store setup would have put a Buy button on a public page and started charging — the change that goes live without anybody deciding it. `PLAN_CHECKOUT` (committed as `0`) is that decision, held separately from the two Lemon Squeezy links for the same reason `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` is a switch rather than an inference: configuring a store and opening it for business are two different acts. With it off, **no host shows a Buy button**, on any page, however many links are filled in — and the tiers are *not* hidden: `/pricing` still lists Free, Pro and Enterprise with their prices, features and the systems each one unlocks, describing where the project is heading rather than what it charges, with every button on the invite. The gate sits in **one place**, `plans.checkout_url`, which is where every surface that offers a plan for money already asks, so a page added later cannot leak a price by forgetting to check a switch. The on/off reading is spelled out rather than left to `bool(os.environ.get(...))`, because the empty string and the word `"0"` are both false strings an operator may write and `bool("0")` is **true** — a switch that turns itself on when somebody writes `off` is the one failure a person cannot see, so `off`, `no`, `false`, `0` and blank all read as off and `on`, `yes`, `true` and `1` as on. The CI warning follows the switch instead of nagging: it fires only in the state that is a mistake — selling switched on with no link to sell through — and stays quiet when buying is deliberately off, because a warning that appears on every push is a warning nobody reads. Committed `0` in `render.yaml` and both documented in `.env.example`, which also lost the two junk `[TEMPLATE]` placeholder lines it had been starting with.
- **💳 A plan a server can actually be on, and a Lemon Squeezy licence that proves it — plus four claims the pricing page was making that nothing could keep**: the store sells two products (Pro ₹499/mo, Enterprise ₹1,499/mo) and the page listed three tiers, and until now nothing connected the two: both paid buttons pointed at the invite page, and buying would have delivered nothing at all. `utils/plans.py` is the missing half — the plan model, and the licence. **A licence is activated per server** (`/bot plan`, administrator, one command with optional options because `/bot` is one of Discord's hundred slots), which is what makes "up to 5 servers" the *store's* rule rather than a number this bot invents and then gets wrong: Lemon Squeezy counts activations against the licence, each server keeps its own row, and releasing one here hands that activation back for somewhere else. A key for a product this deployment does not sell is valid at the store, so it is **handed straight back** rather than left spent, and the refusal names the `LEMONSQUEEZY_VARIANT_*` variable that would fix it; a network failure never downgrades a paying server, and a refused release keeps the record it could not undo, because forgetting a licence the store still counts is how the same seat gets sold twice. Validation uses the store's **public** licence API — no API key is stored and no webhook endpoint has to exist. **Enforcement ships off** (`PLAN_ENFORCEMENT=0`), deliberately: switching it on takes Support Tickets, Economy & Casino, the Market, Starboard and the Activity Tracker away from every server that has not activated a licence — including servers using them today — and holds a free server to one server and no scheduled messages. That is the operator's decision, not a side effect of wiring checkout, so the plans are recorded and displayed and the pricing page says in words that limits are not switched on yet. When it is turned on the withholding is applied in **one place** — `utils/modules.get_states`, which the command gate, the per-server command list and the dashboard all read — so a switch, a picker and a refusal cannot disagree about the same server; the dashboard's single toggle refuses in its own words rather than springing back with "could not save", and "enable all" reaches only the systems the server may have. **The page stopped selling things that do not exist.** The monthly/yearly toggle switched every price to a yearly number with a "Save 20%" badge, and both products in the store are monthly subscriptions — a price nobody could ever be charged, which is the same defect as the dead buttons this page was already rebuilt to remove, one layer further in. Prices are now the store's own (₹0 / ₹499 / ₹1,499) and come from the plan model, so the site cannot advertise a tier the code lacks; both paid cards link at the merchant of choice when the deployment has a checkout URL, and say "No billing yet" when it does not. And **four FAQ answers described a product that does not exist**, each of which reads perfectly plausible: a *7-day free trial of Pro features* on every new server (no product offers one and no code implements one); *"we're setting up our payment processors"* (stale the moment checkout is configured); *"we use encrypted backups"* — the cloud backup is a **raw SQLite file** in a private bucket, and encryption at rest was deliberately removed with the old Gist backup, so the page was contradicting the privacy policy it links to; and a *30-day* post-downgrade retention window that appears in no code path and disagrees with the policy's own 90 days for message history and 365 for activity. Each is now pinned by name in `tests/test_pages_site.py`, because the way they fail is by reading as true. One thing the docs guard caught while this was being written, and it was right to: the checkout variables were being read as `f"LEMONSQUEEZY_CHECKOUT_{plan}"`, which is a name no grep finds — so `.env.example` looked like it documented two variables nothing read. They are spelled out in `CHECKOUT_ENV`/`VARIANT_ENV` now, and the key is only ever shown masked (`38b1…4d51`). **A refusal is not an outage, and the first version of this client could not tell them apart**: the licence API answers a key it has never issued with `404` and the documented falsey body (`{"activated": false, "error": "license_key not found."}`) — measured against the real endpoint, not read off the docs — and every non-200 status was being raised as a transport failure, so a buyer who mistyped one character would have been told *"Could not reach Lemon Squeezy"* and gone to check their connection instead of their receipt. A body carrying the API's own result shape is now returned as the store's answer, while a genuine outage (a `502` and an HTML gateway page, an unreadable body) still reports itself as one. 36 new tests (**1,259 passing**), and `.env.example` plus `render.yaml` declare the four new variables with `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` committed as `0` so the deploy has a definite state instead of a prompt.
- **🧪 The test suite had been failing on GitHub, and the harness it blamed was broken only where nobody had a database**: every `Tests` run since 09:07 was still "in progress" — fourteen of them, the oldest for seventy-six minutes — each parked at `Run test suite` with no log published and no red check, so for an hour and a half the repository looked verified while nothing was. The suite is not slow: it is **1223 tests in 15 seconds** locally, on the same committed code. What was *wrong* is more interesting than the hang, because it was found without it: reproduced the CI shape exactly — a checkout with **no `bot.db` and no `.env`** — and the suite failed two tests, `PreviewHarness.test_the_harness_refuses_what_the_real_endpoint_refuses` and `test_toggling_a_module_in_the_harness_is_reversible`. The cause is that `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` copies the operator's `bot.db` into its temp directory *if it exists*, and a fresh clone, a CI runner and any new contributor have no such file, so the pages were handed an empty SQLite file with **no tables**: `/api/toggle-all` answered `500 no such table: guild_modules`, and the harness's own logs said so (`[MODULES] ❌ Failed to save leveling=False for guild 777001`). Both tests passed on this machine only because it happens to have a populated `bot.db` — the classic shape of a suite that cannot fail in the place it is supposed to run. The fix is to build the schema from the bot's own idempotent `setup()` against the copy when there is nothing to copy, in a thread if the import ever happens inside a running loop; verified by running the whole suite in a clean checkout with neither file present, where it is now **1223 tests, OK** where it was two failures. The workflow is hardened against the *invisibility* of the other problem rather than the guess about its cause: `faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(120, exit=True)` dumps every thread's stack and fails the job if the suite blocks, `timeout-minutes: 20` bounds it from outside, and `concurrency` cancels superseded runs instead of stacking fourteen of them behind a wedged one. The next run's log names the line it is stuck on, which is the diagnostic this could not produce before.
- **🐾 The bot's status is editable now, and the line that was reported is gone**: the presence was a twelve-line tuple hardcoded in `bot.py`, so the only way to change one word was to edit the file and redeploy — and `/bot status` did not exist, which made "remove this one" a code change. The list lives in the database now (`utils/presence.py`), read fresh on every cycle, and **"Knocking over your water glass 🐾" is not in the defaults**: I pinned that with a test that names the line, because a default is exactly where a removed thing quietly comes back on a fresh install. `/bot status` shows the rotation, `text:` adds one, `type:` picks playing/watching/listening/competing, `interval:` sets the minutes, `clear:` turns the activity off entirely ("just be online" is a legitimate choice, not an error), and `reset:` restores the shipped list — one subcommand with optional options rather than five, because Discord gives an application 100 chat-input commands in total and this bot has spent that budget before. **The honest limit, said out loud in the command's own reply: a bot's presence is application-wide.** Discord has no per-server status — an activity belongs to the gateway session, so every server sees the same line at the same moment and a server administrator who could set it would be setting it for every server, last speaker winning. That is the opposite of the *member* profile (nickname, avatar, banner, bio), which Discord does store per guild and which is why `utils/identity.py` can give each server its own name and picture while this cannot; those stay on `/bot avatar` and the dashboard's identity panel. Everything that could be wrong about a value read on a loop for the life of the process is made total rather than left to raise: a hand-edited or truncated setting falls back to the defaults, one bad entry is dropped instead of invalidating the list, an out-of-range interval is clamped (a 0-minute floor is what stops the loop hammering Discord's endpoint), and a failed settings read is reported and retried rather than killing the rotation and freezing the status until the next deploy. The rotation also no longer repeats the previous line, which a plain random choice does one time in eleven and is the only repeat anybody notices. Verified by driving the real command handler: show/add/clear/reset each did what they say, a 200-character line was refused with Discord's 128 named, an unknown type was refused with the valid ones listed, and an empty list cleared the activity rather than conjuring the jokes back. 16 new tests, **1,214 pass on pytest** (`1,216` under `unittest` discovery).
- **🔒 The invite link no longer asks for Administrator — it asks for the seventeen permissions the bot is actually built from**: a public invite is the page's real job, and `Administrator` is the first thing a careful server owner reads before deciding whether to add a bot to their community. It was also the wrong kind of grant: Administrator makes every other bit irrelevant, so a fault in one command is unbounded — it can do anything, anywhere, rather than being limited to what that command does. The list is now explicit and self-documenting (`utils/discord_auth.INVITE_PERMISSION_BITS`), **each entry naming the call site that needs it**: kick/ban/moderate for `/kick`, `/ban`, `/mute` and automod's timeout; Manage Messages for `/purge`; Manage Channels for `/lock`, `/unlock` and ticket channels; Manage Roles for level rewards and `/defaultrole`; Change Nickname for a server's own name and picture; View Audit Log for telling you who added the bot; the two thread bits for the ticket panel and the `/help` panel. **What is left out is as deliberate as what is in**, and was checked against the tree rather than assumed: no `Mention Everyone` (announcements ping a *role*, which needs no permission), no `Connect` (music is gone and nothing joins voice — the tracker reads state changes, which needs only View Channels), no Manage Server, Manage Webhooks, Create Invite, or the member move/mute/deafen bits. The old comment defended the blanket grant as "one number in the URL rather than a long hand-tuned list nobody can check", which is true and was still the wrong trade. Pinned by decoding the bitfield with discord.py rather than comparing it to a literal — and the existing test is the reason this survived so long: it asserted `permissions == str(ADMINISTRATOR)`, so the grant was pinned to *itself* and no test ever asked whether it was the right one. Restoring Administrator now fails by name. README's setup steps no longer tell anyone to grant it, and the table explains what a `Missing Permissions` error in your own server means instead. **1,198 tests pass** (`1,200` under `unittest` discovery), 5 new.
- **🌐 The public site is a static build published to Cloudflare Pages, and the one thing it would have silently lost was the cat cursor**: the front door, the pricing page and the two policies were reachable only through the single Render process that also runs the bot, and that host is a free tier that sleeps — so the pages worth reading *before* you trust a bot with a server were shut whenever nobody had knocked recently. `scripts/build_site.py` renders them into static files and `.github/workflows/site.yml` builds and publishes them. **GitHub Pages was the first choice and is impossible here**, which is worth writing down rather than rediscovering: this repository is private, the owner is on GitHub's free plan, and Pages does not cover that combination (`422 Your current plan does not support GitHub Pages for this repository`). Two runs proved the build was fine and failed on that one restriction — the second after `configure-pages` tried to create the site itself and was told `Resource not accessible by integration`, because creating a Pages site needs repository admin and the workflow token cannot have it. Cloudflare Pages also settles the *reason* for the build: it is served from a CDN and never sleeps, and the rendering stays in Actions because Cloudflare's build image has no Python — the host is handed a finished directory. The part that makes the output work anywhere is that a static host may serve from a subpath, as a GitHub **project** site does at `/<repo>/`: every root-relative URL a template emits (`/static/meow.css`, `/pricing`) points outside the site and 404s, so all of them are rewritten to relative paths, while the pages that genuinely need a process (`/dashboard`, `/login`, `/servers`, the market, `/health`) are rewritten to the live app instead of to a dead path. The build renders through the **real** Flask app's test client rather than a second renderer, so the page on Pages and the page the bot serves are the same template with the same context and cannot drift into disagreeing about the product. **Four defects came out of doing it, three of them invisible from the code.** The cursor was the bad one: a stylesheet's `url()` resolves against *the stylesheet*, and `meow.css` already sits in `static/`, so rewriting `/static/cursor-default.svg` the same way as a page's link produced `static/static/cursor-default.svg` — the server log says it plainly (`GET …/static/static/cursor-pointer.svg → 404`), and the only symptom in a browser is the system arrow, because a cursor image that fails to load is not reported anywhere. It is fixed by giving CSS its own rule (a bare file name, no `static/` prefix) and pinned by a test that resolves the path the way a browser does and insists the file is there. **The live site had no way to add the bot at all**: the templates deliberately *omit* the invite button when no client id is known, and `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` was never set, so the whole marketing page had zero invite links and said nothing about it — the id is public (it is in every invite link anybody can read), it was recovered from the bot's own application, declared in `render.yaml` for the live host and defaulted in the builder for the static one.
