That required the deploy-manifest guard to become *precise* rather than weaker: it forbade any literal `value:` in `render.yaml` on the grounds that it would commit a credential, and it now allows one only for a key on an explicit public allow-list and refuses any key whose own name says `SECRET`, `TOKEN`, `KEY` or `PASSKEY` — proved by feeding it a committed `ADMIN_PASSKEY`, `FLASK_SECRET_KEY`, `SUPABASE_SERVICE_KEY` and `DISCORD_TOKEN`, all four still refused. **A file answered a question about a process.** The landing page's status pill is a claim about the running bot, so the static copy promised *"a sleeping free-tier host takes a moment to wake"* and "live figures appear as soon as it does" — forever, on a host where that is never going to happen. It now says what is true of a static copy and points at the host that can answer. And the **pricing page sold two tiers nobody can buy with two buttons that went nowhere**: "Upgrade to Pro" and "Contact Us" were both `href="#"`, and nothing on the page mentioned that every system it prices is switched on and free in every server today — so it now says so once, above the prices rather than under them, and both buttons do something real. 17 new tests, one of which is the cursor test that found the cursor bug, and the whole suite is green at **1,192 passing on the `pytest` runner**.
- **⏱️ A deploy now posts its own update note within seconds — the boot no longer queues behind the command publish**: every "the update isn't there" has one cause, and it was found by reading the live process rather than the code: `on_ready` **awaited** `sync_commands(bot)` before it started the 25-second cloud backup loop, bound the house channels or posted the release note, and Discord paces command writes hard enough that the live host spent **eighteen minutes** giving one server its 90 commands back (the module-state incident had left Development's picker empty). So for eighteen minutes after a successful deploy there was no update note in any server, no backup of anything earned in that window, and not one line saying so — an update that *had* been deployed read exactly like one that never shipped, which is how it was reported three times. The publish now starts on the bot's own loop as a task and **nothing waits for it**: same compare-first rule, same one-request upserts, same background drain when the daily write budget runs out, same per-server lists — only the waiting is gone. Its outcome is still printed when it finishes (`[SYNC] command publish finished: {...}`), and a publish that cannot even be started is reported instead of quietly skipped, with the coroutine closed so it cannot surface as an unrelated "never awaited" warning. Pinned from both ends: `on_ready` must not await `sync_commands` (and must still start it, on the loop, with its result reported), and the helper itself is driven with a publish that never finishes — it returns immediately, a publish that explodes is reported and never raised into the boot, and a loop that refuses the task answers `None` instead of crashing. **1,174 tests pass on both runners** (`1,176` on `unittest` discovery), 7 of them new.
- **🎨 There is a front door now, and the whole site wears one design system — with the four defects that rebuilding it exposed fixed**: `/` used to redirect, so the first thing a visitor met — a server owner following an invite, anyone reading the link in the README — was a password box for a panel they had never heard of. It now answers three questions in the order they are asked: what this is, what it is doing *right now*, and how to add it. **`static/meow.css` is the whole design system** and every page links it — the dashboard, the sign-in, the picker, the market, the landing page and the two policies — each page's own CSS coming *after* it so a page still wins where it has opinions (the control panel's density is not something the shared sheet should flatten). What it carries: the palette the dashboard already used (so the market stops being a second opinion on what dark looks like — it was `#1a1d23`, the rest of the site `#0b0e13`), the surfaces, the buttons, one focus ring, and an animated aurora of three slow radial washes over a faint grid behind every page. Every animation has a reason — the drift is *place*, the staggered entrance is orientation, the hover lift is an affordance, the status pulse is the gateway answering — and all of it stops under `prefers-reduced-motion`, with `.reveal` explicitly left visible so a suppressed animation can never hide the content. **The two policies are pages now** (`/privacy`, `/terms`): the same files the Discord cards read, rendered by `utils/webdocs.py` through the same `cogs/legal.read_document`, so the web page and the card cannot disagree about which file is the policy — escaping before any markup is applied, no `javascript:` href, and every section present, because Discord's 6000-character budget is a reason to tighten wording rather than a reason a policy should be unlinkable. **And the landing page's figures are only the ones that are true**: servers and members come from the gateway and are *absent* when it has not answered, because on a sleeping free-tier host a confident **0 servers** reads as a bot nobody uses rather than as a process that has not woken up, and the feature cards are the registry's own list so the page cannot advertise a system the bot does not have. `/`, `/privacy` and `/terms` are also the first pages the preview harness serves by **delegating to the real views** rather than restating them, because a harness that describes a route answers differently from the route — which is where every defect found in that file has come from. Four defects came out of the rebuild, all fixed here: the **preview rendered every page unstyled**, because the script runs from `scripts/` and Flask's default static folder was therefore `scripts/static` — the one place the design system could not be looked at, which is the only thing that file is for; the nav's **"Add to Discord" rendered its label in the nav's grey on the accent fill** (about 2.2:1, on the primary call to action) because `.site-nav a` — a class *and* a type — outranks `.btn-primary`, which only looking at the page could have found; the **markdown renderer tore wrapped bullets apart** and printed PRIVACY.md's one emphasis span that crosses a line break as literal asterisks, because it rendered each source line in isolation instead of joining a block first (so a bullet's second half appeared as body text); and the templates could not use `url_for` for the stylesheet at all, because the dashboard and market templates are rendered by the test-suite's own Jinja environment with no Flask context — the literal path is what production serves, and 47 tests said so. Verified by looking at every page in a browser: console clean, every request 200 or 304, no element wider than the viewport, and the label colours measured rather than assumed. 25 new tests, **1,168 pass on both runners** (`1,170` on `unittest` discovery).

- **🔓 Signing in with Discord could hand you to somebody else's site, and the "picture for this server" panel could not accept a picture**: both were found by driving the real surfaces and asserting on what they actually emitted, and both are in the sign-in and upload paths that were added last pass. **The open redirect was a tab.** ``_safe_next`` allowed any string starting with ``/`` that did not start with ``//`` — and the *tab* is stripped when Werkzeug builds the redirect's ``Location`` header, so ``/\t/evil.example`` arrived at the browser as ``//evil.example``: the person completed Discord's sign-in **on this domain** and was then handed to a protocol-relative URL, which is the whole shape of a phishing link. A newline was worse again — the header setter refuses to write the value at all, so the callback **raised and answered 500** *after* the session had already been replaced, i.e. you were signed in and shown an error page. A backslash was the same trick one layer down, because browsers normalise it to ``/`` for http(s). Only a printable, same-site path is a path now, and both outcomes are pinned end to end: the hostile shapes are driven through the real callback (which lands on ``/servers``, not off-site) and every shape of ``next`` is checked, including the ones that must still come through (``/dashboard?guild_id=5``). **The upload was a number, not a bug in the upload code.** ``MAX_CONTENT_LENGTH`` was **16 KB for the whole app**, and Flask checks it *before* any view runs — while ``identity`` accepts images of up to **4 MB**. So a 20 KB PNG was refused by the 413 handler with *"That message is larger than the dashboard accepts (16 KB). Shorten it or split the send."* and a 48-byte one was applied; the feature that lets a server give the bot its own picture could only ever accept a file smaller than a favicon, and the refusal it got was about messages. Only the uploaded file in the suite — 46 bytes — was small enough to pass, which is why nothing caught it. The app-level cap is now the largest legitimate request and each route's real limit is applied **per request**, so the tight 16 KB still holds everywhere else (a 30 KB composer body is refused in the message's own words) while ``/api/identity`` gets a bound that two pictures fit in, with a refusal that names the upload and its size. Everything above was mutation-checked — putting the old ``_safe_next`` back fails five tests by name, and putting the single 16 KB cap back fails the two upload tests — and **1,143 tests pass on both runners** (`1,145` on `unittest` discovery), with the live dashboard re-driven in the browser afterwards: console clean, every request 200, and an 11 MB body refused as an upload rather than as a message.
- **🪞 The preview was serving a page from before the edit, and the composer's counter claimed tags it did not have**: two defects of the same family — an instrument that reports something other than what is there. Flask defaults `TEMPLATES_AUTO_RELOAD` to `app.debug`, which the preview harness leaves off, so a long-lived preview held the **bytecode-cached** template: change the page, reload, and the browser shows the old one — the failure mode where a fix is verified against a page that never received it, and which this pass hit for real (an edit was read back as absent from a server that had started before it). The harness now rereads the template per request, which was watched rather than assumed: a sentinel written into `templates/dashboard.html` appears on the next fetch with no restart, and disappears again when it is reverted. The second was the composer's own counter, which said **`0 / 2000 incl. tags` on an empty box** — a claim about a count that has nothing in it, inviting the reader to hunt for tags that are not there. It now names the tags only while tags are actually in the total (`0 / 2000` empty, `5 / 2000` with text, `27 / 2000 incl. tags` with one chip), and still says nothing in embed mode, where the tags ride in the content and the server counts them separately — the same rule `_build_message` applies. Both are pinned: the guard that forbids the template from stating a limit of its own caught the first draft of this fix, because the reasoning comment quoted the limit it was reasoning about, which is exactly the class of drift it exists to prevent; **1,133 tests pass on both runners**, `bot.db` untouched (mtime 12:20 against a 14:12 clock), and the live page re-driven afterwards — empty, text-only, tagged, embed, and the 26th tag refused with the bot's own *"At most 25 tags per message."* — console clean, every request 200.
- **🎭 The preview answered "SENT" to messages the bot refuses, and let its queue pass the cap it was reporting to the page**: driving the dashboard as its first user would — a 2,100-character body pasted in, then a tag added — produced a receipt reading *"SENT 13:24"* where the bot answers *"That message is 2100 characters; the limit is 2000."* and posts nothing. The page had even marked its own counter `counter over`; nothing acted on it. This is worse than an unfaithful preview: the harness is the instrument a defect is *found* with, so one that reports success invents a working feature and hides the real one behind it. Both composer endpoints now call the **real** validator (`keep_alive._build_message`) instead of restating it — the same reason the scheduling endpoints already call `_schedule_timing` — so the harness cannot disagree with the bot about channels, tag counts, a title with no embed, or length; the refusals are compared word for word in a test rather than trusted to a shared import, and one of them is reachable *only* through a tag (a body of 1,990 characters plus one resolved mention is 2,013), which is exactly the case a `maxlength` attribute does not prevent. Two more doors of the same lie were closed with it: the scheduling route could **queue** a message the bot refuses to queue (it hand-rolled its own checks, and had no length rule at all), and the queue accepted past the `max_pending` it was handing the page — filling it to *"32 of 25"* with 429s never returned. A generated message is the one kind with no text to validate, so the builder is deliberately **not** run for it, exactly as the real route does not: the first version of this fix refused the only scheduled message that writes itself, which is the same defect from the other side — refusing what production accepts. Reported, not changed: with the queue full the page prints "25 of 25" and its Schedule button stays enabled, so the limit is learned from the toast rather than before clicking; the toast does name the cure. Verified through the browser after the fix: the over-limit send now shows *"Could not send: That message is 2100 characters; the limit is 2000."* with the body still in the box, a generator scheduled with nothing written still succeeds ("Weekly server digest — built when it posts"), and the 26th schedule is refused with *"cancel one before adding another"*. 1,131 tests pass on both runners.
- **🫥 The command panel said "Every command the bot has is offered on this server" directly above the thirty-one names that were not, and the preview let the same system be on in one panel and off in the next**: two defects the same shape — a page describing a state it was not in. The note is a `.field-head`, so `display: flex`, and `display: flex` outranks the browser's own `[hidden]` rule; the rule guarding this family already existed for four other elements and this one — the newest — had been missed, so the attribute was set by the server's render *and* by the live refresh and the browser ignored both. It is now guarded by class rather than by that one id, so the next `.field-head` is covered too, and the guard test reads the **template source** as well as one render: an element hidden by `{% if … %} hidden{% endif %}` only carries the attribute in the renders where its condition is true, which is how a fixture whose state has nothing hidden could not see it. Restoring the missing rule fails that test by name (`.field-head`). The harness (`scripts/preview_dashboard.py`) had the second version of the same lie: its switches flipped their own in-memory stub and wrote nothing down, so the module cards said *Economy on* while the command list and the All-servers page — both reading the real store — said *off*, and only the stub was believed. It now writes through the real `utils.modules` (safe by design: the process works on a copy of the database made at import), which makes every panel agree and makes the preview show what production would instead of what it was seeded with — driving Economy off through the page now moves **all** of them: the switch, the panel's 90/0 to 59/31, and the All-servers line to "10 of 11 systems on · off: Economy & Casino". Its two bulk endpoints do the same, and "disable all" reaches every toggleable module rather than the three cards on screen, which is the state an operator is actually in when the bot looks broken. One test had to stop reading the harness's seed values as truth: it now renders first, so its baseline is what the page shows, and names a server no other test switches — a baseline of seed values is right only by accident once another test has legitimately changed the store. Driving the All-servers page found a third: its **Manage** button posted to `/servers` and was answered with the same page — "this harness holds no session, so there is nothing to switch" — so the one control for opening another server visibly did nothing. The harness now keeps which server it is in, redirects the way production does, and the picker says **Open** where you already are and **Manage** on the rest; an id it is not in is refused in production's own words at its own status. 1,129 tests pass on both runners, and the checkout's `bot.db` is untouched (mtime 12:20 against a 13:17 clock).
- **🗂️ Every server now has its own command list, and the bot can be renamed and re-pictured in one server without touching another**: Discord reaches a command to every server an application is in, and offers **no** way to narrow that per server — no permission bit, context or option hides a global command in one guild while another keeps it. So a server that turned the market off kept `/market` in its picker and kept being told the market was off when a member clicked it, which is what "make the commands separate per server" was actually about. The tree is now registered **per server**, from that server's own module state: turn a system off and its commands leave that server's picker; turn it back on and they come back. Three pieces make that real rather than theoretical — **the dashboard republishes a server's list when a switch flips** (a module toggle *is* a command-set change; the writes happen on the bot's own loop, and a burst of switches costs one publish, not eleven), **a server added while the bot is running publishes on join** (the shared scope no longer carries the tree, so without this a new server would sit with an almost-empty picker until a deploy), and `force_sync.py` — the operator's own tool — now publishes each server's filtered list instead of the whole tree. The rule itself is one pure function (`utils/command_scope.py`), read by the publisher, the tool and the panel so they cannot disagree. **Discord's global scope keeps only what has to work with no server to resolve it** — the help panel and the policies, the two commands a member can run in a DM — and the copies of everything else that were still registered there are removed by a migration with one guard that matters: only once **every** server has published its own list with nothing pending. A failed publish followed by a successful delete would leave that server with no commands at all. Nothing is ever removed by name-absence: a command may only be taken out of a scope that the feature registry declares and that the scope's own modules turn off, so a typo upstream can leave a command visible but cannot delete one somebody is using. `sync_commands`'s old "guild lane" (and its nine skipped tests) come back as the only lane, this time because a per-server list is the feature rather than a duplicate of what the global scope already carried. **For safety, two defects the invariant behind this change found**: `cogs/tracker.py`'s `/activity` commands checked **nothing** — the listeners that *collect* activity honoured the toggle, the commands did not, so a server with the tracker off kept all three while reading rows nothing was writing any more; and `/dbstatus` was refused whenever the **moderation** module was off, because it was missing from the four names `cogs/server_control.py` exempts from that cog's gate — asking whether the database is healthy has nothing to do with moderation. Both are fixed, and the invariant is now a test: *every command a server loses is one the bot would have refused there* — the real cogs are loaded, every module switched off, and each command's own `interaction_check` called, so the hidden set and the refused set must be the same set in both directions. That test is what found both defects. **A server can also put its own name, picture, banner and bio on the bot**: Discord keeps those four on the bot's **member** in each guild (`PATCH /guilds/{id}/members/@me`), so they are per server by construction and nothing in the new panel can reach the application profile — `/bot avatar` and `/bot banner` stay the operator's and stay global, which is the containment the global profile is missing today. Who may: a one-time dashboard link is minted by the `/help` panel for an **administrator of one server** and is scoped to it, so that session changes its own server and no other (pinned: a request naming another server lands on its own); the passkey is the operator's and may do the same anywhere. Images are sniffed and measured before Discord is asked (a sentence naming PNG/JPEG/GIF/WEBP instead of a bare 400), the length limits are Discord's own, a save is applied to Discord **before** it is recorded so a refusal cannot leave the panel showing a name the server does not have, only the fields actually submitted are sent (a nickname change cannot clear a picture), and the stored picture is served from our own record rather than hot-linked, which the page's own CSP would block. The dashboard carries both panels and reads the command-scope numbers from the live endpoint rather than guessing, so the panel and the picker cannot drift. Verified in the browser against the real template: the save posts multipart to the real route and surfaces the harness's honest refusal, and switching Economy off moved the panel from 90 offered / 0 hidden to **59 / 31** — the 20 economy commands plus the 11 that need economy *and* another module at once. **Two more harness fixes** (`scripts/preview_dashboard.py`), both the shape this file keeps producing: its command list was built from the features' own `gate_commands`, which omits the commands several modules gate together, so it claimed the bot had 79 commands where it has 90; and the identity panel was rendered disabled, which hid the form's own error path from the one tool that exists to look at it. A live `force_sync.py --dry-run` confirmed the per-server filter against the real application — the home server would receive all 90, and the Development server 7 — and is also the reason the migration itself was **not** run from this checkout: the local `bot.db` still holds the all-off module rows from the incident two passes earlier, so publishing from here would have stripped 83 commands from that server. The deploy restores the current backup, and the bot publishes from that state on its next boot. 1,059 tests pass (was 992; 34 new for the identity store and endpoint, 24 for the scope rule and the publisher, and the nine guild-lane tests are un-skipped rather than replaced).
- **🔐 A server's own administrators can now open the dashboard and see only the servers they run, and the bot can be added to another one from the page**: both existing ways in belonged to the operator — a one-time link is scoped to the one server it was minted in, and the passkey is the whole bot — so neither could answer the question every other bot's dashboard answers: *which of my servers is this bot in, and which of those may I manage?* That answer depends on the person, so the dashboard grew a third way in. **Sign in with Discord** asks for `identify` and `guilds` and nothing else (no email, no connections, no messages), and the rule lives in one pure function (`utils/discord_auth.py`): you see a server when you hold **Manage Server** or **Administrator** or own it *and* the bot is already there — the intersection, because a server the bot has left has nothing to configure and a server you cannot configure is Discord's refusal arriving later and less clearly. `permissions` arrives from Discord as a **string**, so the filter parses it; comparing it to an int would keep every owner and drop every administrator, which reads as "the list is just short" rather than as a bug. **The session is scoped, not merely filtered**: `_resolve_guild_id` gained a `user` branch that checks every request against that person's list, recomputed from the bot's live guilds each time — so a request naming somebody else's server is served their own, a server the bot has left or the person was demoted from stops being reachable immediately rather than when the cookie expires, and `/servers` refuses a hand-posted `guild_id` by name ("You do not manage that server.", 403) instead of switching. `state` is single-use and compared in constant time, because the callback is a URL anybody can make a browser follow; `next` is accepted only as a same-site path, so `//evil.example` cannot turn sign-in into an open redirect; and the access token is used for two lookups in the same request and never stored — the session keeps a Discord id, display name, avatar URL and the server list, and nothing about the person reaches the database. **The bot can also be added to another server from the dashboard**: `invite_url()` builds Discord's own authorize URL with `bot`+`applications.commands` (without the second scope the invite produces a bot with no slash commands at all, which the README already documents as a support case), offered in the header and on `/servers`, with `guild_id`+`disable_guild_select` when a specific server is meant. `/servers` is also where somebody who manages nothing lands: an empty state that says so and offers the invite, rather than a dashboard showing a server they cannot open. **With no client id and secret the method is simply off** — the login page says which two variables are missing and prints the exact callback URL to register for the host it is running on, because an unregistered redirect URI is the most common way this feature "just says no" and Discord's own message reads like a bug in the bot. The passkey and one-time link are untouched, and a passkey session is not offered the picker (it already reaches every server). Docs move with it: the Privacy Policy's flat "no profile information is requested" is now false and says what signing in actually shares, `TERMS.md` says your Discord permissions are the credential (so removing somebody's roles removes their access here), `.env.example` documents both variables and the redirect URL to register, and `docs/architecture.md` answers both new questions. The policy had to be **tightened wording for wording** rather than appended to: Discord renders the whole document on one card inside a 6000-character budget, `test_legal.py` fails if any section falls off it, and the new facts put it 1,070 characters over — so the same facts are stated in fewer words everywhere, both documents carry the same new effective date (another thing that test checks), and the file now fits with room to spare. 47 new tests, and the ones that matter were watched to fail: the string-permissions case, the intersection, the other server's page, the forged form, and the replayed state.
- **🧹 This checkout's database now agrees with the deploy, which it had not for two days**: the local `bot.db` still held all eleven modules switched **off** for the Development server — the rows `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` wrote during the incident — while the backup the deploy actually restores holds none off. Read side by side rather than assumed, the local copy was the odd one out, so it was refreshed from the newest Supabase snapshot (`restore_from_cloud`) and both servers now read **90 of 90** commands from here. The safety copy of the old file sits beside it as `bot.db.before-refresh-2026-09-19` (`.gitignore` now covers `bot.db.*`). Correcting the record while doing it: `force_sync.py` was **never** the destructive path this changelog warned about — it upserts and retires named commands and never applies the per-server removal rule, so a run from a divergent checkout would have published a short list into one server's scope and left every command visible, wasting writes rather than taking commands away. The boot is the only path that removes the stale global copies, and it runs on the deploy, from the restored database.
- **⏱️ Eleven module switches now really do cost one publish, and the coalescing that protects the write budget is tested at last**: the dashboard's **Disable all** flips every module in a row and each flip asks the bot to republish that server's commands, so a burst is coalesced behind a short settle window — but the pass cleared its own "this server changed" flag *before* waiting, which booked every click that landed during the wait as a fresh change and bought a second full pass over every scope for a state that had not moved. The flag is now cleared after the wait, so the wait absorbs the burst while a switch that flips once the publishing has started still gets its own pass. Measured rather than argued: eleven clicks 10 ms apart produce **one** publish that carries the state the clicking left behind, a click landing mid-publish produces exactly **two** (the second carrying the new state), and a second click while one is already queued starts no second task. None of that had a test — only the dashboard's *call* to a stubbed hook was pinned — so the burst, the mid-publish click and the refusals (a server the bot is not in, a bot whose loop is not running, no server named) are pinned now, and clearing the flag in the old order was watched to fail the burst test with `2 != 1`. Everything else was driven against a fake application that models Discord's two scopes as the API does — per-scope commands with the id a delete needs, reads shaped as `AppCommand` objects carrying the server-only keys and **omitting the ones still at default** (the shape that once made every unchanged command look changed), and a server's commands shadowing a global one of the same name — and asserted on **what a member would see in the picker** rather than on what we stored. Starting from the live application's real state (all 90 commands global, nothing per server, which is exactly what the real application holds today), the migration registers each server's own list, leaves every server seeing all 90, trims the shared scope to `help` and `legal`, and then: a module switched off in one server removes its commands from **that** server only; switching it back on returns them; a second boot with nothing changed costs **zero writes and zero deletes** (the 200-a-day budget is spent on real changes only); a server added while the bot runs publishes its own list on join; a server whose publish fails leaves the shared scope **untrimmed**, so no server can lose commands to a half-finished migration; the commands queued behind a refused one still register; and with every module off a server keeps exactly `help` and `legal`, which are structurally undeletable because they are the two names the feature registry does not declare. 33 checks across two harnesses, all of them passing after the fix. Verified through the dashboard's own surface too: the live panel read **90 offered / 0 hidden**, matching the rule computed independently in Python, then **59 / 31** the moment Economy was switched off through the page — the same numbers, with the same 31 hidden names, that `command_scope` computes for that state.
- **🐞 `/bot bug` failed with `50035 — Invalid Form Body … placeholder: Must be 100 or fewer in length`, so the report form never opened for anyone**: the command's whole job is to open one modal, and Discord refuses a text input whose placeholder runs past 100 characters — at the moment the modal would appear, not when it is built — so `/bot bug` and the `/help` panel's 🐞 button both answered *"An unexpected error occurred"* and the reporter had no way to file anything. The string was `BUG_REPORT_HINT`, **122 characters** long, passed to `discord.ui.TextInput` by name so nothing about the call site looked wrong; it now reads *"What happened, and what you expected instead? The command you ran, the steps, and any error text."* at 97, and the shape of the failure is checked rather than the one string: `tests/test_static_health.py` measures every `placeholder`, `label` and `description` handed to a `TextInput`, `Select`, `SelectOption` or `Button` in the live tree against Discord's own cap for that component, resolving a module-level constant as well as a literal, because the value that broke was a constant and a comparison against the literal alone would have missed it. Both facts were confirmed against the object the bot actually sends: the real modal, built through the real code path, serialised to the payload Discord receives — `components.0.components.0.placeholder`, character for character the path the 50035 error named — now measures 97. The guard is mutation-checked by putting the 122-character hint back, which fails it by name. Reported, not changed: the same sweep looked for a second instance of the class and found none — the only other computed component strings are a trivia answer option (longest 12 characters, against 80) and the ticket category names (longest 32, against 100).
- **📈 Nobody could reach the market pages from the dashboard, and the preview 404'd on the first click of the link that now goes there**: the charts are served at `/market/<symbol>`, and **nothing** in any template linked to them — the only way in was a member's `/market` command in Discord, so an operator reading the control center had to already know the URL. The header now carries **📈 Market** beside the server switcher, the same tab and the same page family. Driving it in the browser turned up three more dead ends, all in `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` and all the same shape as the `/` 404 fixed one pass earlier: the new button lands on `/market`, which production **redirects** to the first instrument and the harness answered with Flask's 404; an unknown symbol rendered a bare `<h1>404 nope</h1>` where production writes "404 — nope is not on the market" and offers BTC, so a misspelt symbol (a thing people do) taught nothing; and the header's own **Sign out** button 404'd, because the harness serves no session — both `/login` and `/logout` now answer a page saying exactly that, with a way back, rather than a wall. What held up under careless hands, re-checked after the fix: sending with nothing ticked and nothing written (“Pick at least one channel first.”, no request at all), three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued (**one** POST, one receipt, the button back to "Send message"), a junk channel filter (“No channel matches “zzzz”.”), a schedule time in the past (refused by name, no request), a time 49 hours out ("in 2 days", which is the floor-to-nearest fix from the previous pass still holding), and the market page under the real CSP with the chart drawn and “live · prices from preview · updated 12s ago” — every request 200, the only console entry the intentional 404 of the bogus symbol. The decisive check of the database fix was made through the browser rather than read: clicking the preview's **Disable all** turns its own three switches off and leaves **production's eleven modules enabled** — the exact click that caused the outage last pass.
- **🩺 Every gated command in the main server answered *"the X system is currently turned off"* — switched off by the dashboard preview, which was writing the live database**: all eleven modules in the Development server were off, so `/balance`, `/rank` and every other gated command replied that the system was turned off *on this server* while the same commands worked everywhere else — a bot that looks broken in exactly the server its owner was looking at. Nothing in Discord had switched them off: the eleven rows in `guild_modules` were written seconds apart on 17 September, by `scripts/preview_dashboard.py`. That harness is the documented way to eyeball the dashboard, it imports the real `utils.db`, and the pages it serves write through it — module toggles, `/api/toggle-all`, scheduled messages, channel bindings — so one click on its bulk toggle in a playthrough was enough to turn all eleven off, and the bot running on the same checkout uploaded that database as the **production backup**, which is what the Render deploy restored. Two fixes, one of them outside the code: the state is repaired (all eleven modules back on for Development, through the real `/api/toggle-all` so the change persists *and* schedules its own backup, then read back off the live page the operator sees), and the harness now copies `bot.db` into a temporary directory before `keep_alive` is imported, so what a person clicks in the preview is discarded with the process and the live file is never opened. The copy is made before the import because the import is what reads the path — the same ordering trap that made a test's database patch useless once. Reported, not changed: the 3,680 fixture rows already in `scheduled_messages` are inert and prune themselves under the existing 90-day history window.
- **🧪 The suite was writing into the operator's database, and both runners now run somewhere else**: the same `bot.db` held **3,680** rows in `scheduled_messages` for guild `777001` and channels `555001` — fixtures from `test_scheduler.py`, the one database-touching test file that does not use `tests.support.TempDatabaseMixin`, because `db.setup()` and every insert went through the *default* path. It was not contained: the bot running on this checkout uploaded it on its 25-second cloud loop, so developer fixtures are in the production backup and in the live instance. `tests/support.py` already stated the rule — nothing in this suite may touch a file inside the project — but a guard each test has to remember to use is not a guard, and the fix had to be a file **both** runners load: the check in `.github/workflows/tests.yml` discovers with `unittest`, which never looks at a `conftest.py`, so the redirect lives in `tests/__init__.py`, which is imported before any module inside it by pytest and by discovery alike. The suite now builds its schema in a temporary directory, and after a full run of each runner the operator's `bot.db` is **byte-for-byte unchanged** (`sha256` compared before and after) — which is the claim, rather than a reading of the code. `tests/test_db_isolation.py` pins all of it: the suite's path is outside the project, `cloud_sync` resolves the *same* file it does (a backup of a different file is how this data got uploaded in the first place), the `unittest` runner gets the guard too, and the preview harness — checked in its own process, because importing it would move this process's database — reports a path outside the project while leaving the live file's size and mtime untouched. 4 new tests (`984 → 988`).
- **🔢 The command picker was missing the two newest commands and still offering twelve dead ones, and every boot said nothing about it**: `/bot` and `/legal` were in the tree and could not be created, while `/dashboard`, `/updatedb`, `/sync`, `/setavatar`, `/setbanner`, `/addlevelrole`, `/removelevelrole`, `/privacy`, `/tos`, `/meowfee`, `/activity-top` and `/activity-channel` were still registered even though the code had folded them away — so typing `/botdb` matched nothing, which is the screenshot this started from, while `/updatedb` offered a door that no longer exists. Discord read back **exactly 100 chat-input commands**, its ceiling, and twelve of them were dead: `RETIRED_COMMANDS` — the *only* list that may delete a registered command, because the publisher can never infer a removal from a missing local name without risking the destructive bulk overwrite this project already lived through — still named just the three `upload` commands, so the folds left their commands registered forever. With no slot free, creating a new command is refused with ``30032 Maximum number of application commands reached``. Two more faults hid it: `_publish_scope` returned on the **first** refusal, so the three legitimate updates queued behind the create (`activity`, `eco-admin`, `levelroles`) never went out either, and the boot line printed only `published 0, 32 still pending` — the reason was sitting unprinted in the sync's own `detail` field, and the only automatic retry was scheduled for a *spent write budget*, so the identical silent no-op repeated on every boot. All three are fixed: the twelve folded names are on the retirement list (with `/activity` deliberately left off — the group *is* that name now, so deleting it would take the group with it), a refused command no longer stops the ones after it while a spent daily budget still stops everything (that window is closed for every command), and the boot prints the reason and, for `30032`, names the cure. `force_sync.py` — the documented path for a terminal — now retires too, which it did not, which made it unable to free a full scope at all; it also waits out a short `429` rather than leaving a command undeleted. Verified live against the real application: the tool retired all twelve and published `/bot` and `/legal`, and Discord now holds **exactly the 90 commands the tree has** — `/bot` with its six subcommands, `/legal` with `privacy`/`tos`, `/activity` as the group, no folded name anywhere — leaving ten slots free. The ceiling was the diagnosis and not a theory: the creates that had failed on every boot succeeded the moment twelve slots were freed. 7 new tests (`973 → 980`), and the old pinned assertion ("exactly the upload commands") is the one that had to change.
- **🔧 Every command in the bot failed at random, and neither host log named the cause: two copies of the bot were online at once, and a command that breaks left no trace anywhere the operator reads**: `/balance` answered *The application did not respond*, the same command had worked an hour earlier, and `#real-time-cmd-data` — the channel built to show exactly this — stayed empty. The host logs said `40060 Interaction has already been acknowledged` and `10062 Unknown interaction`, which is the signature of a *duplicate* bot rather than a broken one: Discord delivers an interaction to **every** connected session of one application, so with a copy running on the Mac and the deploy running on Render each command was handled twice — whichever copy answered first won, the other raised *already been acknowledged*, and an interaction that reached a session that had gone quiet was answered too late to be accepted at all, which is what a member sees as "the application did not respond". The second copy was not a guess: the backup lease in Supabase named it (`srv-daiqibp594qs739qebc0-hibernate-…`, refreshed every two minutes). Nothing in either log pointed at it, so three things are fixed. **The boot now says it out loud**: the check that already stops two copies fighting over the *backup* (`cloud_sync.other_live_copy`, off the event loop) is asked the same question about the *gateway*, and a boot that finds another live copy posts one card in the operator's own `#bot-error` naming the other copy, the symptom in the member's words, and which copy to stop — this one, since the copy holding the database is the one keeping everyone's progress. A lease that cannot be read, an empty bucket and a stale lease all answer "nobody", because a false alarm here is worse than silence and "I cannot tell" must never stop a legitimate boot — and the first deploy after this change proved the point: the container that came up **reported the pod it had just replaced as a second copy**, telling the operator to stop the deployment they had just made. Render gives every container its own hostname and a rolling deploy overlaps the two, so the lease names "another instance" that is the same deployment's previous pod; a sibling of this container's own service is now logged and not announced, while a laptop beside a deploy — the case that actually breaks commands — still is, and the backup's own rule is untouched, because refusing to write while the previous container may still be writing is what the lease is for. **A failed command is written down now**: `on_app_command_completion` is dispatched only in the `else` branch of discord.py's own call, so a command that raised never reached the log at all — the empty channel read as "nobody uses this bot" while the bot was in fact broken, which is the exact inversion the log exists to prevent. `command_failed` posts the missing entry — the command, the runner, the server, the channel and the **reason**, unwrapped from `CommandInvokeError` — and it never touches the interaction, because the failures worth recording are precisely the ones whose answer already failed; only a *Discord* error carries its message, while a Python-level failure keeps its type alone, since a message raised while handling what somebody typed can quote it back and this log promises never to copy that. **And a failed command is answered now**: `cogs/tracker.py`'s three `@…​.error` handlers ended with `raise error` for everything that was not a permission failure, and discord.py calls a command's own handler *before* `tree.on_error` without guarding it — so the tree's handler never ran, the interaction was never answered, and the member got silence for every failure that was not a missing role. They answer the permission case (through `style.send_card`, so a response that is already done cannot raise a second time) and leave the rest to the tree's handler, and a static rule over the live tree fails the suite if any command error handler re-raises again. Verified against the real thing rather than argued: both new cards were posted to the real `#real-time-cmd-data` and `#bot-error` through the REST API on a client that **never opens a gateway session** (so the copy that is still running could not lose an interaction to the check), read back component by component — `-# /balance` / `## ❌ Command failed` / `Reason: NotFound — 404 Not Found (error code: 10062): Unknown interaction`, and the duplicate card naming the Render copy and the fix — and deleted again, leaving both channels empty. 22 new tests (`951 → 973`).
- **🗓️ A message scheduled two days out said "in 1 day", and the preview answered its own documented address with a 404**: the composer was driven in a browser through the flows its first real user would attempt, careless versions included, and one sentence in it was wrong. `scheduleWhen` truncated to the whole unit — `Math.floor` on days, hours and minutes — while the time picker stores **minutes**, so "the same time in two days" is really 1 d 23 h 59 m away and floored to **"in 1 day"**: a full day early, on the one number that decides whether an announcement gets double-booked, printed both in the confirmation toast and on the queue row. It now rounds to the *nearest* unit, which is wrong by at most half a unit where truncation was wrong by a whole one, and a boundary lands exactly on the next unit up (23 h 59 m is "1 day", not "24 hours"). Four boundary cases pin it, at two days, one day, an hour and half an hour, and reverting to `Math.floor` was watched to fail it. The rest of the pass held up under hands: sending with nothing ticked, sending nothing at all, three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued (one POST — the button reads **Sending…** and refuses re-entry), five on a module switch (one request, right end state), the bulk buttons' confirmation, a junk channel filter, a schedule time in the past, cancelling and firing from the queue, a reload **during** a send (the history row is written before the answer, so the page comes back with the send recorded and nothing double-posted), a message of 2500 characters, a typed `@everyone` (inert by construction: the endpoint builds `allowed_mentions` from the tag picker, never from the text), and the tag path end to end — a chip added, sent, and the stored payload read back as `mentions: ["@everyone"]`. Two suspicions were chased and dismissed by reading the surrounding code rather than the diff: an over-limit send *is* refused, on the second check that measures the assembled content including its tags, and a counter that read 31 for one `@everyone` chip was counting a 21-character draft the browser had restored into the textarea. What was actually broken was `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` again, and in four places: **opening the port it prints answered Flask's 404**, because the harness served `/dashboard` and never `/`, which is the first step of its own usage line; the **market page drew a permanent "offline — could not reach the bot"** over a page whose every request had answered 200, since the stubs returned `{"quotes": {}}` and `{"candles": []}` and the page's `renderQuotes` threw on the object while its `catch` blamed the network; the **server switcher navigated to `?guild_id=` and the harness ignored the query**, so picking the second server came back showing the first with the select snapped back — a control that visibly does nothing, in the one tool whose job is showing what the page does; and the **generator picker offered a single option**, because `schedule_content_choices` was never passed, so the one kind of scheduled message that writes its own text could not be reached from the preview at all. All four are fixed and pinned: `/` answers the liveness line for a monitor and redirects a browser (as production does), the market stubs are now built by **`quotes_payload` and `series_payload` themselves** so the preview cannot drift from the keys production sends, `/dashboard?guild_id=` renders the server that was asked for and falls back to the first on an unknown id exactly as `_resolve_guild_id` does, and the generators come from the registry the endpoint validates against. Each was mutation-checked (the floor put back, the old stub shapes put back, the route renamed away, the guild id hardcoded) and each reported the failure. The 60-point series is labelled **observed**, not exchange OHLC, because the page prints that label and inventing traded prices is the claim this project keeps removing. 5 new tests. Reported, not changed: the harness serves no `/login` or `/logout`, so its visible **Sign out** button is a dead end (it is a render harness with no session, and pretending otherwise would teach nothing); its send stub reports success for a channel the real endpoint refuses; and it re-implements 24 endpoints by hand, which is where all four of these defects came from.
- **🔍 Driving the dashboard in a real browser found the harness lying, not the page**: the composer was put through the careless versions of its own flows — sending with nothing ticked, sending nothing at all, three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued, five on a module switch, a junk channel filter, a schedule time in the past, cancelling everything, and a message full of `@everyone` — and it held up: each dead end answers with a sentence naming the fix ("Pick at least one channel first.", "Write something to send.", "That time has already passed — pick a time in the future."), the send button reads **Sending…** and refuses re-entry so three clicks make **one** post, every switch has a pending guard, a typed `@everyone` cannot ping (the endpoint sends `allowed_mentions` built from the tag picker, never from the text), the over-limit counter goes `over` at 2000, and an emptied queue says "Nothing queued. Write a message, choose Schedule, pick a time — it is posted even if this page is closed" rather than showing a blank box. What the same pass *did* find broken was `scripts/preview_dashboard.py`, the documented way to eyeball this page: it had **no `/api/toggle` or `/api/toggle-all` route**, so every module switch and both bulk buttons were answered with Flask's 404 page — the page showed its red "Request failed (404)" toast and the switch sprang back, which is the dashboard's headline control looking broken in the one tool that exists to check it. Both routes (plus `/__last-toggle`, matching the harness's existing `/__last-send`) are now served, a missing module is refused the way the real endpoint refuses it, and a test reads the page and the harness and fails if the page ever calls an endpoint the harness does not serve — mutation-checked by renaming the two routes away and watching it report them. The same pass corrected the harness's seed data, which omitted the channel `name` from a *successful* receipt: real rows carry one, and seeding one without made the page's id fallback look like a defect in a browser when it is in fact the honest answer for the only two records that can lack a name ("I am not in that server", "That channel no longer exists"). Reviewing that work adversarially turned up three more: the two new harness routes answered an unknown module **404** where the real endpoint answers **400 "Unknown module"** (a stub that lies about the refusal teaches the page's error path the wrong thing, so both routes now match ``keep_alive``'s status and words, including "No guild available"); the operator-log setup card was called **inside the boot loop's single `try`**, so a failure on a cosmetic card would have skipped binding every server after it — the exact inverse of that loop's purpose, and it now has its own guard; and fuzzing every entry point of `utils/operator_log.py` with a client whose lookups raise, a guild whose name is ``None``, a member whose display name is ``None`` and a command whose qualified name is ``None`` found ``announce_setup`` **raising** where its siblings returned ``False`` — its guard had covered the send but not the lookups that find the channel to send to. All four are pinned, the fuzz is now a test (no entry point may raise, whatever it is handed), and the setup card was re-posted to its real channel afterwards and read back to confirm the restructure did not change what it says.
- **📊 The operator can now see who has the bot and what people actually run with it**: two channels in the bot's own server were named for this and fed by nothing. `#active-servers` now receives a card every time a server **adds** the bot — its name and id, its size, its owner, and who invited it (read from the server's audit log when View Audit Log allows, and honestly marked *unknown — needs View Audit Log* when it does not, which a fresh invite has not granted yet) — and a card every time one **removes** it, which is the same event whether the bot was kicked, the server deleted, or the bot banned, plus how long it had been there. `#real-time-cmd-data` receives every command anyone runs anywhere: the command, the person, their id, the server, the channel and the time. It hooks **completion**, not `on_interaction`, because a command that failed never completes and a log that counted attempts as uses would read as healthy while the bot was broken. A server added *while the bot is running* is set up on the spot rather than waiting for a restart: the join handler binds that server's own house channels by name and sends it the release note too, and those three steps are isolated from each other so a channel list it cannot read does not cost it the note or the log entry. Both channels belong to the operator by construction — `roles_for` refuses `guilds` and `cmdlog` on any other server, so a member's server cannot bind them even by naming a channel to match, and nothing this module writes ever lands in a member's own server. Three rules, each from a failure this project already had, and each pinned by a test: an entry may never break the thing it logs (a missing channel, a refusing channel and an unreadable audit log are all reported and swallowed — a command that just worked must not fail on its way into the log); an entry may never ping, which is not theoretical since a **server's name is a stranger's text** and `@everyone` is a legal server name — the real payload was read back to confirm it carries `AllowedMentions(everyone=False, …)`; and **what a person typed is never copied into the log** (a `/bug` report, a `/pay` note, a `/purge` reason are the person's, and the command, its runner and its outcome are what the log is for). Verified live against the two real channels: a join, a leave and a command each posted once with the right fields — server and member as plain name-plus-id, never a raw mention — and all three were deleted again, leaving both channels empty. Both halves of the first run are stated rather than assumed, because a feature that is installed and writes nowhere looks exactly like one that works: the boot that binds these channels posts a **one-time card in the channel itself** saying what arrives in each (and, if one of the two is missing, naming the channel to create), and the first entry that finds nothing bound reports **where the operator already looks** — the update channel, then the error channel, then the log channel — naming the channel to create, that `/bot db` binds it without a restart, and that the notice is sent *once per channel, not once per entry*, so it cannot bury the channel it is trying to save. With nothing bound anywhere it says so on the host once and stays quiet rather than looping. The removal card also stopped implying a reason it does not have: Discord reports a kick, a deletion and a ban as one event, and the card now says exactly that instead of letting the reader guess. Both cards were read back out of the live channels with Discord's own renderer before being deleted, which is how two faults in this pass were caught: the setup card came out **silent on a real first run** because it consulted the store for bindings it had just been handed (a write that had not landed would have cost the one card whose whole job is to explain the channel), and the notice interpolated a whole sentence into a slot meant for an entry name, printing "I had a one card each time a server adds the bot to write". 28 new tests, and removing the two calls from `bot.py` was watched to fail four of them.
- **📜 The policies now describe the bot that is actually running, and the operator's half is written down**: both documents were dated 16 September and had fallen behind — they predated the dashboard composer and its scheduler, the `/bot bug` path, and the move from an encrypted Gist to a private Supabase bucket. Version 2 of [`PRIVACY.md`](PRIVACY.md) and [`TERMS.md`](TERMS.md) fixes the claims that were simply wrong and adds the ones a member cannot discover for themselves: a bug report **leaving its server** for the bot's own when the server has no bug channel, and carrying the reporter's mention and server name with it; announcements composed in the dashboard — including the ones **scheduled** to post later — stored with their text, target channels, repeat rule and who queued them; the dashboard's own footprint (the `meow_session` cookie, HttpOnly / SameSite / Secure on HTTPS, up to 8 hours, the single-use 15-minute sign-in link, and a login rate limiter that counts failures **per address in memory only**, never in the database); the two retention windows (365 days of activity, 90 days of queued-and-sent message history) rather than one; and the backup's real protection — a private bucket holding **raw** `bot.db`, so the service-role key is the database's password and there is no second layer to promise. The third-party list was checked against the code rather than copied forward: the market's seven providers and the chart page's **unpkg.com / cdn.jsdelivr.net** are named, and the font CDN that no page has loaded since the dashboard dropped webfonts is gone. Three operator documents were missing entirely and now exist: [`docs/legal/README.md`](docs/legal/README.md) (what is served where, and the rule that these documents are contracts with the code), [`docs/legal/data-requests.md`](docs/legal/data-requests.md) (the store-by-store procedure for an access or deletion request, including the two traps that make the obvious version wrong — a boot restores a backup, so a local deletion can be undone, and a snapshot written before the request still holds the data), and [`docs/legal/sub-processors.md`](docs/legal/sub-processors.md) (every outbound service, what it receives, and how to drop it). One piece of stale configuration came out with them: `.env.example` still offered `GITHUB_BACKUP_TOKEN`, `GIST_BACKUP_ID`, `BACKUP_ENCRYPTION_KEY` and two cadence variables for a Gist path that had been deleted — telling an operator their database was encrypted at rest while the Privacy Policy said otherwise. A test now fails if the template documents a variable no code reads. All of it is pinned to the code it describes: the retention numbers come from `tracker.RETENTION_DAYS` and `scheduled.HISTORY_DAYS`, the cookie and link lifetimes from `keep_alive`'s own config, the CDN from the page that loads it, and the documents must still fit Discord's render budget with their last line arriving on the card — because a policy a member cannot read is not a policy. Each new guard was mutation-checked (the dead backup variables put back, the retention window mis-stated) and reported the failure; 18 tests where there were 8. Reported, not changed: the dashboard's CSP still allows a font CDN that nothing loads.
- **🐞 A bug report is a message again, and there is now a command for filing one that an administrator can reach**: reporting a fault in the bot was spelled two ways and both were wrong. The surface people actually reached for — the ticket panel's own *Bug Report & Glitch* category — opened a **private ticket channel**, which is a support conversation with one member, not a report the operators can see; and the `/help` panel's 🐞 button only worked in a server that had happened to create a channel named `#bug`, refusing everywhere else with "this server has no bug-report channel", which reads to whoever filed it as the bot losing their report. So `#bug` stayed empty. Reporting is now one path: the report is a **card in a channel** with a thread on it, never a ticket — the ticket panel is left as it was for members' own problems — and it is asked for in this order: the server it was filed in, then the **bot's own server** (`BOT_SERVER_GUILD_ID`, defaulting to the server whose `#update`, `#bot-error` and `#bug` exist for exactly this). The first target that accepts the post ends the walk, so one report is never posted twice, a channel that refuses falls through to the next, and a channel deleted since the panel was posted no longer strands the report. The bot's own server is looked at *by name* when no binding exists there, because a report must not be refused merely because a boot has not happened yet — the only rule reused is the one the binder itself uses, nothing is created, and a server that renamed its own channel still gets what it bound. `/bot bug` is the new administrator command (`/help`'s button stays open to everyone, since finding a bug is not an operator privilege), it reports as itself in the card's `Filed via` field, and the card names the server it came from — so a report that arrives in the bot's own server still says which server it was filed from. Verified against the live servers with a real post: a report filed in Hmm resolves to Development's `#bug` (bound or not), posts exactly once with `Server: Hmm` and `Filed via: /bot bug`, creates no ticket channel, and was cleaned up afterwards. 19 new tests, driving the real modal and the real resolver, including the case that produced the complaint: a server with no bug channel of its own.
- **✂️ Three command slots came back and no feature did**: `/dashboard`, `/addlevelrole` and `/removelevelrole` were spending three of Discord's hundred on doors the bot already offered somewhere better. The dashboard's single-use sign-in link is minted by the **⚡ Open Dashboard** button on the `/help` panel an administrator already opens — the same 15-minute token, scoped to the same member and server — and the level-role verbs are one group: `/levelroles view` for anyone, `/levelroles add` and `/levelroles remove` behind Manage Roles, each audience still declared separately in the one permission table. The tree is now **90 of 100** top-level commands, ten free, where it was at the ceiling and one command away from Discord refusing the next one outright. Folding them turned up the defect the folds hide best: the panel was still *advertising six commands that no longer existed* (`/meowfee`, `/activity`, `/activity-top`, `/activity-channel`, `/privacy` and `/tos`, each folded away earlier), which a member reads as "unknown command" with nothing in any log — the text now matches the tree, every "run `/dashboard` in Discord" instruction (the login page's own error states, the boot log, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `docs/`) names the button instead, and a test walks the panel and fails if it ever names a command Discord does not have — mutation-checked by putting the three stale names back and watching it report all three. Merging `/lockdown-server` into `/lockdown` was the obvious fourth saving and is deliberately **not** taken: those two carry different audiences (Manage Channels versus Administrator), the permission table declares one audience per command, and merging them would either hand a channel moderator a server-wide lockdown or make the table stop describing what is actually enforced. One trade is taken knowingly: `/levelroles add` and `/levelroles remove` were hidden from members without Manage Roles as top-level commands, and as subcommands they are now *offered* and refused by the check instead — which is what all 39 subcommand audiences declared in that table already do, because discord.py writes `default_member_permissions` into the payload only for top-level commands (`Command.to_dict` skips it when a parent exists). Enforcement is unchanged and driven by the suite, which calls every gated command's own checks with an empty permission set and asserts it refuses; the gap is in how the tree is published, it predates this change, and it is worth its own fix.
- **🚀 `/bot push` — one command that puts an update live, and says which of the two things it did**: nothing inside Discord could ship a change. A bot process cannot commit — what is running on the host was built from the repository — so the command has exactly two honest shapes, and it never confuses them. With a Render Deploy Hook URL in `RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL` it POSTs the hook and hands the build over, and the boot that follows announces the release to every server by itself, which is why the command deliberately does *not* also post the notes (that would double them). With no hook it says plainly that no code moved and posts this version's notes there and then — read from the changelog that shipped *with* the running code, so they describe what is actually live. Either way a card goes out **before** anything is attempted and is edited with the outcome, because a deploy restarts the process and a record that was never written is the one failure this command must not have; it reports in the server's `#test-ground` when one is bound — the channel an operator is watching while they test — falling back to the update channel, and its reply maps each outcome (`posted`, `up_to_date`, `unbound`, `unreachable`, `failed`) to a sentence instead of a shrug. The hook URL is a credential, so it is never printed anywhere: a failure reports the status and the exception's *name*, not the URL that exception was quoting back.
- **🧩 The bot's own commands now cost one command slot instead of four**: `/setavatar`, `/setbanner`, `/sync` and `/updatedb` are `/bot avatar`, `/bot banner`, `/bot sync` and `/bot db`. Discord allows a hundred top-level commands per application and this bot was using exactly a hundred, so the next command anyone wanted would have been refused outright — the same wall that once stopped this cog from loading at all. A subcommand nests inside its parent and costs none of that budget, so the group leaves three slots free (97 of 100) and a test now fails if the tree ever closes that gap again. `#test-ground` also joins the house channels as `test` — decorated names still bind — and `/bot db`'s "still unbound" hint is built from the binder's own name table now, instead of a hand-written list that could fall out of step with it.
- **🔁 Updating a card now reaches Discord, so a failure that repeats is counted onto it instead of posted again**: `utils.style.edit_card` built its Components V2 payload with `embed=None` beside `embeds=[]`, and discord.py rejects a payload carrying both — by *key*, not by value — so every V2 edit raised `TypeError` and dropped into the legacy retry, which Discord refuses outright on a message that is already a card. The first caller this reached was the new repeat-counting on the error channel: the second occurrence of a failure could not be counted onto the card in the channel, so a second card appeared instead. The payload now carries exactly one embed keyword, a card that genuinely cannot be edited reports the real error instead of the rejection it provoked, and the starboard's star-count edits — failing silently the same way on every V2 card — are fixed by the same change. Caught by driving the live API: a real error card now reads *Seen **2** times since …*.
- **📍 Decorated channel names bind anyway, and a server added later gets its ping**: binding compared channel names exactly, so the home server's own `💬・bot-cmd` was invisible to it and a server that never ran `/updatedb` had nowhere to put level-up cards; names are now matched on their letters and digits too, with an exact name still winning over a decorated one. The release note's ping was one role id belonging to the home server, so a second server received the note with no mention in it; that id still wins where the server has it, and any other server is matched by role name (`Dev`, `Admin`, …) instead. Verified against both real servers: `#💬・bot-cmd` binds, and the new server's note pings its own `Dev`.
- **🛡️ A mis-set environment variable can no longer take the bot offline, and the health endpoint stopped claiming a disconnected bot is ready**: `ENABLE_MESSAGE_CONTENT_INTENT=1` beside a Developer Portal that has not granted Message Content makes Discord reject the *entire* identify payload, so the bot never connects at all — the app stays up answering HTTP while every command is silent, and the only clue is one line in a host log nobody reads. The bot now asks Discord what it has actually been granted (the `/applications/@me` flags) *before* asking for anything, and starts without an intent the portal has not enabled, naming what it dropped and which toggle brings it back; it never adds an intent nobody asked for. Separately, `/health`'s `bot_ready` came from a flag set once inside `on_ready` and never cleared, so a bot that had since dropped off the gateway still read as ready — the opposite of useful when the question is whether it is online. The endpoint now also answers a live `gateway` field (`connected`, `reconnecting`, `never_connected`) from `is_ready()`, keeping `bot_ready` for the monitors that already read it. The error channel no longer posts a card per failure either: repeats of the same failure are counted onto the card already in the channel ("Seen **7** times since 19:52 UTC") for ten minutes, so one broken command cannot bury the channel that exists to report it, and a card that can no longer be edited is forgotten so the next failure posts fresh instead of counting into a void. `/updatedb` now re-binds the house channels it can find by name and says which roles are still going nowhere — the one way to do that without a restart. 28 new tests, two of which caught bugs written in the same sitting: the intent flag check shifted its bits twice and read as "nothing was ever granted" (which would have silently dropped the members intent as well), and the failure card had lost the line naming who ran the command.
- **📍 A second server is now a first-class server: every guild says where the bot talks, and it listens**: four of the bot's house channels each pointed at one guild or at nothing at all. The update channel was a literal in `bot.py` naming the home server's, so a server added later never received a release note; the failure path knew only a process-wide `ERROR_WEBHOOK_URL`, which cannot say *which* server a command broke in; `bot_cmd_channel_id` was written by `/updatedb` and read by leveling, but nothing ever bound it, so every server but the home one posted **no level-up cards at all**; and `#bug` had nothing behind it. `utils/bot_channels.py` is now the one place that knows: `update`, `error`, `bug`, `commands` and `log` are per-guild settings, filled from channel *names* the first time a server is seen (`#update`, `#bot-error`, `#bug-report`, `#bot-cmd`, `#logs`, most specific name first) and authoritative from then on — so renaming `#bot-error` tomorrow does not quietly move where the bot reports. Binding runs for every guild in `on_ready` and is idempotent: the first boot after this deploy sets a new server up with no command and no dashboard visit, and every boot after it changes nothing. The deploy digest now fans out **per server**, in that server's own channel, pinging the dev role only where that role exists, and each server's "already told" record is its **own** row — so a server whose channel is unreachable retries by itself instead of dragging the others into a re-announcement it already showed them, and a server that has never been told still gets its first note instead of inheriting one it never saw. The pre-existing process-wide record is handed to the server it describes, once, so the server that has been reading these notes all along does not suddenly see its whole changelog again. An unexpected command failure also lands in that server's error channel, naming the command, who ran it and the error, with the webhook reporting unchanged. `#bug` gets a producer without costing a command slot the bot does not have: the existing `/help` panel carries a **🐞 Report a bug** button that opens a modal, posts the report to the guild's bug channel and opens a thread on it, so the discussion stays next to the report. Verified by driving the real announcement with two stand-in servers: each is told exactly once in its own channel, the role is pinged only where it exists, a second boot announces nothing, and a server whose send Discord refuses keeps the note it is still owed while the readable server is told; the same run under the old single shared record leaves the second server with **nothing**, which is the bug this fixes. 38 new tests, including one that fails if any single channel name could bind two roles.
- **🔔 A scheduled message that does not go out now tells someone, in the place the server already watches**: every other part of a scheduled message was engineered to be trustworthy — what gets posted is what was stored, the claim is exactly-once, a repeat keeps its own slot — and the *failure* of it was the one part with no audience. It was written down on the row and shown in a panel somebody has to open, which means a daily announcement that quietly stopped was invisible until someone wondered why it was quiet; the person who queued it was recorded and never told anything. A delivery that did not arrive now posts a card in the guild's **log channel** — the same `log_channel_id` the ticket ratings and moderation cases already use — tagging the person who queued it and nobody else: **⚠️ did not go out** with the reason, the channels it was meant for, the message itself, and what happens next (**"it tries again 09:00 — failure 1 of 3, then it stops"** for a repeat that still has attempts, **⛔ "has been stopped"** once it has given up, **⚠️ "part was refused"** when most of the announcement arrived and one channel did not, **⏭️ "was skipped"** for a run too late to be worth sending, and **"interrupted by a restart"** for one left mid-flight, which is the case where a half-posted message may already be sitting in the channel). A message that *did* arrive is not news — it is visible where it landed — and a generator with a quiet week to report is not news either, so neither posts anything. With no usable log channel the person who queued it is **DM'd** instead, and only when neither is possible does it become a line in the bot's own log; a card that cannot be delivered is caught, so one unreadable alert can never cost you a delivery. Verified against the gateway stand-ins by driving the real delivery loop: a failure posts exactly one card naming the channel and pinging the queuer, a partial send keeps the channel that refused it, a repeat that failed once says when it tries again, the third failure says it stopped and stops counting 0-of-3 for a partial, a quiet week and a successful send post nothing, and re-running the loop does not post a second card. A card built from a 5,000-character error is checked against Discord's own embed limits field by field, because an alert the API refuses is a failure nobody is told about — the exact thing this exists to prevent. 12 new tests, and the alert call, the success-is-not-news rule and the DM fallback were each removed in turn to watch their test fail. Your server's log channel was read back out of the live backup to confirm the card has somewhere real to go. Reviewing it turned up four things, all fixed: the restart sweep now takes **ownership** of each interrupted row with a conditional write (the same "the affected row count is the answer" idiom the delivery path uses), so two instances sharing a database cannot each post a card for one interruption; the sweep reports what the store **recorded** rather than assuming, because an interrupted *repeat* is not finished — it keeps its next slot, and the card says so instead of telling you to queue it again; the DM fallback asks Discord for that one id rather than trusting a member cache that holds only people the bot has met (the Members intent is off), which is what makes "the person who queued it is told" true rather than likely; and a skipped or interrupted run no longer reads as *"failure 2 of 3"*, which implied the run had counted against a streak it does not touch.
- **🧾 Everything the dashboard posts is written down, not only what it schedules**: **Send now** was the one path whose result had nowhere to live — the per-channel report was handed to the page as JSON and died with the tab, so a message the bot sent as itself left no trace beyond a `print` into an in-memory ring that goes with the process. The panel's **Recently sent** list therefore held the scheduled half of what the dashboard had posted and silently omitted the rest, which meant the sends that matter most had no answer an hour later: *which* channels got that announcement, did any of them refuse it, and who sent it. Every immediate send is now filed as a finished row in the same table — a new `source` column, added additively so a live database (and every cloud-restored copy of it) upgrades on boot — carrying the channels, the per-channel ✓/✕ with the reason, who sent it, and the moment. It is written as `sent`/`failed` and **never `pending`**, so the delivery loop cannot find it and post it a second time; it never counts against the 25-message queue cap; and the 90-day prune retires it like any other history row. The list marks the difference — a **`sent now`** pill for an immediate send, the repeat for a queued one — and refreshes as soon as a send lands, so the record is there without a reload. One fault fell out of the same surface: **the receipt's retry footer was on screen with nothing to retry** (`Retry the 0 failed channels`, under a clean send), because `display: flex` outranks the browser's own `[hidden]` rule and this was the one hidden element in the page with no guard — the page already carried three for exactly this reason.
